     1→# Requirements: CipherBox v1.1 IPFS Infrastructure
     2→
     3→**Defined:** 2026-03-07
     4→**Core Value:** Zero-knowledge privacy -- files encrypted client-side, server never sees plaintext
     5→
     6→## v1.1 Requirements
     7→
     8→Requirements for IPFS infrastructure milestone. Each maps to roadmap phases.
     9→
    10→### IPNS Reliability
    11→
    12→- [ ] **IPNS-01**: Self-hosted Someguy deployed alongside Kubo, replacing delegated-ipfs.dev as primary IPNS routing provider
    13→- [ ] **IPNS-02**: IPNS resolution uses DB-first strategy with async Kubo DHT verification via self-hosted Someguy
    14→- [ ] **IPNS-03**: Recovery tool resolves IPNS via self-hosted Someguy instead of delegated-ipfs.dev
    15→- [ ] **IPNS-04**: System degrades gracefully when DHT resolution is slow (timeout + DB fallback within 2s)
    16→
    17→### Vault Migration
    18→
    19→- [ ] **VAULT-01**: rootFolderKey embedded in IPFS vault blob v2 format (ECIES-wrapped in blob header)
    20→- [ ] **VAULT-02**: Client reads rootFolderKey from IPFS blob on login, falls back to DB vaults table
    21→- [ ] **VAULT-03**: Lazy migration writes vault blob v2 on next folder metadata publish
    22→- [ ] **VAULT-04**: encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey column deprecated from vaults table (HKDF-derivable)
    23→- [ ] **VAULT-05**: Recovery tool updated to parse vault blob v2 format
    24→- [ ] **VAULT-06**: Desktop app (Rust) parses vault blob v2 format
    25→
    26→### BYO-IPFS
    27→
    28→- [ ] **BYO-01**: RemotePinningProvider implements standard IPFS Pinning Service API (pin/unpin/status)
    29→- [ ] **BYO-02**: DualPinProvider pins to both CipherBox node and user's configured node
    30→- [ ] **BYO-03**: Per-user IPFS config stored server-side (endpoint URL, encrypted auth token, provider type)
    31→- [ ] **BYO-04**: Settings UI for configuring custom IPFS node endpoint and credentials
    32→- [ ] **BYO-05**: Connection test endpoint validates user's IPFS node is reachable and API-compatible
    33→- [ ] **BYO-06**: All IPNS publishes still route through CipherBox API regardless of BYO config
    34→- [ ] **BYO-07**: Quota tracking becomes advisory for BYO users with clear UI indication
    35→
    36→### Performance Baselines
    37→
    38→- [ ] **PERF-01**: IPFS/IPNS duration histograms added to Prometheus (publish, resolve, pin, cat)
    39→- [ ] **PERF-02**: API endpoint p50/p95/p99 baselines defined per critical route
    40→- [ ] **PERF-03**: Kubo Prometheus endpoint scraped for node health metrics (peers, bandwidth, datastore)
    41→- [ ] **PERF-04**: TEE republish batch duration histogram added
    42→- [ ] **PERF-05**: Client-side timing instrumentation for encrypt/decrypt, upload/download, IPNS operations
    43→- [ ] **PERF-06**: End-to-end user journey timing captured (login-to-vault, upload-to-visible, share-to-accessible)
    44→- [ ] **PERF-07**: k6 load testing scripts simulating concurrent users (upload, download, publish, resolve)
    45→- [ ] **PERF-08**: Capacity thresholds documented with scaling recommendations
    46→

    49→Deferred to future release. Tracked but not in current roadmap.

I've successfully read all 10 pending todo files. Here's a summary of their full contents:

## Pending Todos Summary

### 1. **2026-02-07-web-worker-large-file-encryption.md**
Offloading large file encryption to a Web Worker. Files >= 10MB would use a dedicated worker to prevent UI blocking. Files < 10MB continue on main thread. Considerations include handling Transferable objects and fallback to main thread on worker errors.

### 2. **2026-02-14-erc-1271-contract-wallet-authentication.md**
Adding support for smart contract wallet authentication via ERC-1271. Current implementation only verifies EOA signatures. Requires an RPC connection to Ethereum mainnet for on-chain signature verification. Key questions: free vs paid RPC provider, cost/latency implications.

### 3. **2026-02-20-desktop-auto-update.md**
Integrating Tauri's auto-update plugin to check GitHub Releases for new versions and prompt users to update. Requires code signing for security verification. Currently users must manually download DMG and replace the app.

### 4. **2026-02-21-move-root-folder-key-to-ipfs.md**
Moving `encryptedRootFolderKey` from server storage into an IPFS blob pointed to by the root IPNS record. This eliminates all server-side crypto material storage and makes the server a pure relay. Key concern: adds IPNS resolution to critical login path.

### 5. **2026-02-21-desktop-tee-enrollment-for-new-files.md**
Files created on the desktop FUSE mount are not enrolled with TEE for automatic IPNS republishing. Solution: wrap the IPNS private key with TEE public key and include in publish payload, similar to web app behavior.

### 6. **2026-02-21-ipns-resolution-alternatives.md**
Investigating alternatives to the unreliable `delegated-ipfs.dev`. Options: self-hosted Kubo node, alternative delegated routing providers, hybrid approach, IPNS over PubSub, direct CID caching, or DNSLink. Success criteria: sub-2s resolution and >99.5% availability.

### 7. **2026-02-22-crdt-ipns-inbox-sharing.md**
Research on using CRDTs over IPNS for decentralized share discovery. Proposes a per-recipient "inbox" IPNS address where sharers publish encrypted invitations. CRDTs (G-Set or OR-Set) solve write-conflict issues. Challenges: write access control, IPNS reliability, revocation, state size growth.

### 8. **2026-02-24-async-incremental-search-index.md**
Making search index build async/incremental for large vaults. Current `buildFromFolderTree()` is synchronous and rebuilds entire index on every palette open. Three options: (1) yield to event loop with batches, (2) Web Worker, (3) incremental updates. Option 3 is ideal long-term.

### 9. **2026-02-26-alternative-mfa-factor-types.md**
Adding alternative MFA factor types beyond device shares and recovery mnemonic. Priority options:
- **Passkey (WebAuthn PRF)** — best, fully non-custodial, hardware-backed
- **Password-derived key** — simplest to ship
- **Secondary OAuth** — moderate complexity
TOTP/SMS/Email not viable without server custody.

### 10. **2026-02-14-bring-your-own-ipfs-node.md**
Adding user-level IPFS configuration to let users bring their own IPFS nodes or pinning services. Key design questions: client-direct vs server-relay upload, per-user config storage, provider abstraction, IPNS implications, quota tracking, fallback/reliability, and conflict detection implications for Phase 16.

     1→---
     2→created: 2026-02-21T00:00
     3→title: Investigate alternatives to delegated-ipfs.dev for IPNS resolution
     4→area: api
     5→files:
     6→  - apps/api/src/ipfs/ipfs.module.ts

    11→
    12→## Summary
    13→
    14→## Context
    15→
    16→CipherBox relies on `delegated-ipfs.dev` for IPNS resolution (both server-side and in the recovery tool). This service is unreliable — known 502 errors have been observed, and there's a DB-cached CID fallback to work around it. If the rootFolderKey is moved to IPFS (see sibling todo), IPNS reliability becomes even more critical.
    17→
    18→## What to investigate
    19→
    20→- **Self-hosted IPFS node with DHT:** Run our own Kubo node for IPNS resolution instead of delegating to a third-party service
    21→- **Alternative delegated routing providers:** Are there other public or paid delegated routing APIs beyond delegated-ipfs.dev?
    22→- **Hybrid approach:** Self-hosted primary, delegated-ipfs.dev as fallback
    23→- **IPNS over PubSub:** Faster resolution via pubsub instead of DHT, if both publisher and resolver are connected
    24→- **Direct CID caching:** Continue the current DB-cached CID pattern but make it the primary path, with IPNS as background refresh only
    25→- **DNSLink:** Use DNS TXT records as an alternative IPNS resolution mechanism for the root vault
    26→
    27→## Relationship to CRDT-based sharing research
    28→
    29→Solving IPNS reliability is a prerequisite for the CRDT-based IPNS inbox approach to serverless share discovery. If IPNS becomes reliable, the same CRDT-over-IPNS pattern could replace centralized workarounds across folder sync, sharing, and device registry. See `.planning/todos/pending/2026-02-22-crdt-ipns-inbox-sharing.md`.
    30→
    31→## Success criteria
    32→
    33→- Identify at least one alternative that provides sub-2s resolution latency and >99.5% availability
    34→- Evaluate cost, maintenance burden, and infrastructure requirements
    35→- Recommend whether to self-host, use an alternative provider, or improve the caching strategy
    36→

     1→---
     2→created: 2026-02-21T00:00
     3→title: Move rootFolderKey to IPFS vault record, eliminate server-side key storage
     4→area: crypto
     5→files:
     6→  - packages/crypto/src/vault/types.ts
     7→  - packages/crypto/src/vault/init.ts
     8→  - apps/api/src/users/users.service.ts
     9→  - apps/web/src/services/folder.service.ts
    10→  - apps/web/public/recovery.html
    11→---
    12→
    13→## Summary
    14→
    15→## Context
    16→
    17→`EncryptedVaultKeys` stores two ECIES-wrapped keys on the server:
    18→
    19→- `encryptedRootFolderKey` — randomly generated AES-256 key (cannot be derived)
    20→- `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` — deterministically derivable via HKDF (redundant)
    21→
    22→Since the root IPNS private key is derivable from the user's secp256k1 key, the only reason EncryptedVaultKeys exists is to store the root folder key.
    23→
    24→## Proposal
    25→
    26→Move `encryptedRootFolderKey` into the IPFS blob pointed at by the root vault IPNS record. The blob would contain both the ECIES-wrapped folder key and the AES-GCM encrypted FolderMetadata. This eliminates all server-side crypto material storage.
    27→
    28→New flow: login → derive IPNS key via HKDF → resolve IPNS → unwrap folder key from blob → decrypt metadata.
    29→
    30→## Considerations
    31→
    32→- **IPNS reliability:** Adds IPNS resolution to the critical login path. If resolution fails, user can't get their root folder key. Server-cached CID fallback mitigates this but doesn't eliminate the risk.
    33→- **Breaking change:** New wire format for root IPFS blob. Requires version bump or migration strategy per `docs/METADATA_EVOLUTION_PROTOCOL.md`.
    34→- **Recovery tool:** Must be updated to handle the new blob format.
    35→- **TEE republishing:** Unaffected — TEE uses separately wrapped IPNS keys from `folder_ipns` table.
    36→- **Zero-knowledge improvement:** Server stores zero crypto material, becoming a pure relay.
    37→
    38→## References
    39→
    40→- `docs/METADATA_SCHEMAS.md` Section 10 (EncryptedVaultKeys)
    41→- `docs/METADATA_EVOLUTION_PROTOCOL.md` Section 3.2 (Breaking Changes)
    42→
```

     1→---
     2→created: 2026-02-14T00:00
     3→title: Add bring-your-own IPFS node support
     4→area: api
     5→files:
     6→  - apps/api/src/ipfs/ipfs.module.ts
     7→  - apps/api/src/ipfs/providers/ipfs-provider.interface.ts
     8→  - apps/api/src/ipfs/providers/local.provider.ts
     9→  - apps/api/.env.example
    10→  - apps/web/src/lib/api/ipfs.ts
    11→---
    12→
    13→## Problem
    14→
    15→Currently CipherBox uses a self-hosted Kubo node for IPFS, configured server-side via environment variables (`IPFS_LOCAL_API_URL`). There is no way for end users to bring their own IPFS node — the choice is made by the server operator, not the individual user.
    16→
    17→For a zero-knowledge privacy tool, users may want to:
    18→
    19→- Pin their encrypted data to their own IPFS node (self-sovereignty)
    20→- Use a preferred pinning service (e.g., web3.storage, Filebase, nft.storage)
    21→- Run a personal Kubo/IPFS node at home and have CipherBox pin directly to it
    22→- Avoid relying on a third-party pinning service entirely
    23→
    24→This requires a user-level IPFS configuration (not just server-level env vars) and potentially a hybrid model where the backend can relay uploads to a user-specified IPFS endpoint, or the client uploads directly to the user's node.
    25→
    26→## Solution
    27→
    28→TBD — key design questions to resolve:
    29→
    30→1. **Client-direct vs server-relay:** Should the web app upload encrypted blobs directly to the user's IPFS node (bypassing backend), or should the backend proxy to the user's configured endpoint?
    31→   - Client-direct: simpler, no server load, but CORS/connectivity issues
    32→   - Server-relay: consistent quota tracking, but server sees user's IPFS credentials
    33→
    34→2. **User settings model:** Add per-user IPFS config (endpoint URL, auth token, provider type) stored in user settings. UI for configuring custom node in settings page.
    35→
    36→3. **Provider abstraction:** The existing `IpfsProviderInterface` (pin/unpin/fetch) is already well-abstracted. A new "user-custom" provider that reads config from user settings rather than env vars would slot in cleanly.
    37→
    38→4. **IPNS implications:** If user pins to their own node, IPNS republishing and delegated routing need to work with the user's node or be handled differently.
    39→
    40→5. **Quota tracking:** If uploads go directly to user's node, server-side quota tracking becomes optional or advisory.
    41→
    42→6. **Fallback/reliability:** What happens if user's node is unreachable? Fallback to default provider, or fail?
    43→
    44→7. **Conflict detection:** Phase 16 (M2) adds API-level optimistic concurrency for conflict detection — the API checks IPNS sequence numbers on publish. BYO-IPFS bypasses the API for publishes, so this conflict detection mechanism will stop working. This feature will need a client-side conflict detection strategy (e.g., IPNS resolution before publish, or CRDT-based metadata) when BYO-IPFS is implemented.
    45→
```

     1→# Roadmap: CipherBox v1.1 IPFS Infrastructure
     2→
     3→## Overview
     4→
     5→CipherBox v1.1 transforms the platform from "IPFS as a storage backend with database fallbacks" to "IPFS-native with the database serving only auth." The milestone establishes performance baselines before making changes, replaces the unreliable delegated-ipfs.dev dependency with self-hosted IPNS resolution, migrates rootFolderKey to an IPFS vault blob (achieving true zero-knowledge server), adds BYO-IPFS node support for data sovereignty, and completes performance baselines with client-side instrumentation and load testing after all features are stable.
     6→
     7→## Milestones
     8→
     9→- **v0.1 Staging MVP** - Phases 1-10 (shipped 2026-02-11)
    10→- **v1.0 Production** - Phases 11-17.1 (shipped 2026-03-05)
    11→- **v1.1 IPFS Infrastructure** - Phases 18-22 (in progress)
    12→
    13→## Phases
    14→
    15→**Phase Numbering:**
    16→
    17→- Integer phases (1, 2, 3): Planned milestone work
    18→- Decimal phases (2.1, 2.2): Urgent insertions (marked with INSERTED)
    19→
    20→Decimal phases appear between their surrounding integers in numeric order.
    21→
    22→- [ ] **Phase 18: Performance Instrumentation** - Server-side Prometheus histograms and Kubo metrics scraping to establish baselines before any architectural changes
    23→- [ ] **Phase 19: IPNS Resolution Improvement** - Replace delegated-ipfs.dev with DB-first resolution and self-hosted Someguy, achieving sub-2s resolution with >99.5% availability
    24→- [ ] **Phase 20: Vault Migration** - Move rootFolderKey to IPFS vault blob v2 format, making the server store zero crypto material
    25→- [ ] **Phase 21: BYO-IPFS Node Support** - User-configurable IPFS pinning endpoint with dual-pin strategy, Settings UI, and connection testing
    26→- [ ] **Phase 22: Performance Baselines Completion** - Client-side timing instrumentation, end-to-end journey timing, k6 load testing, and capacity documentation
    27→
    28→## Phase Details
    29→
    30→### Phase 18: Performance Instrumentation
    31→
    32→**Goal**: Operators can observe IPFS/IPNS latency and API performance in Prometheus/Grafana before any architectural changes are made
    33→**Depends on**: Nothing (first phase of milestone)
    34→**Requirements**: PERF-01, PERF-02, PERF-03, PERF-04
    35→**Success Criteria** (what must be TRUE):
    36→
    37→1. Prometheus exposes duration histograms for IPNS resolve, IPNS publish, IPFS pin, and IPFS cat operations with per-operation labels
    38→2. API endpoint response times are captured at p50/p95/p99 for all critical routes (auth, IPNS resolve/publish, file upload/download, folder CRUD)
    39→3. Kubo node health metrics (peer count, bandwidth, datastore size) are visible in Prometheus via scraped Kubo endpoint
    40→4. TEE republish batch duration histogram captures per-batch timing with success/failure labels
    41→   **Plans**: TBD
    42→
    43→### Phase 19: IPNS Resolution Improvement
    44→
    45→**Goal**: Users experience reliable, fast IPNS resolution without dependency on external delegated-ipfs.dev service
    46→**Depends on**: Phase 18 (baselines must exist to measure improvement)
    47→**Requirements**: IPNS-01, IPNS-02, IPNS-03, IPNS-04
    48→**Success Criteria** (what must be TRUE):
    49→
    50→1. Self-hosted Someguy is deployed alongside Kubo and serves as the IPNS routing provider, with delegated-ipfs.dev fully removed from the resolution path
    51→2. IPNS resolution completes within 2 seconds in the normal case (DB-first with async DHT verification) and degrades gracefully to DB-only when DHT is slow
    52→3. The standalone recovery tool resolves IPNS records via self-hosted Someguy without depending on the CipherBox API or delegated-ipfs.dev
    53→4. No user-visible errors or stale metadata when the DHT is temporarily unreachable (DB fallback serves correct data)
    54→   **Plans**: TBD
    55→
    56→### Phase 20: Vault Migration
    57→
    58→**Goal**: The server stores zero crypto material -- rootFolderKey lives in the IPFS vault blob, making the server a true zero-knowledge relay
    59→**Depends on**: Phase 19 (IPNS must be reliable before making it a login-adjacent dependency)
    60→**Requirements**: VAULT-01, VAULT-02, VAULT-03, VAULT-04, VAULT-05, VAULT-06
    61→**Success Criteria** (what must be TRUE):
    62→
    63→1. New users get vault blob v2 format on first login, with rootFolderKey ECIES-wrapped in the blob header and readable without any database round-trip
    64→2. Existing users are lazily migrated to vault blob v2 on their next folder metadata publish, with the DB copy retained as a permanent fallback
    65→3. The standalone recovery tool parses vault blob v2 and extracts rootFolderKey without needing the CipherBox API or database
    66→4. The desktop app (Rust) parses vault blob v2 and uses the embedded rootFolderKey for FUSE mount initialization
    67→5. The encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey column is deprecated from the vaults table (HKDF derivation is the canonical path)
    68→   **Plans**: TBD
    69→
    70→### Phase 21: BYO-IPFS Node Support
    71→
    72→**Goal**: Users can configure their own IPFS node for data sovereignty, with files pinned to both CipherBox and their personal node
    73→**Depends on**: Phase 19 (stable IPNS resolution benefits BYO workflows)
    74→**Requirements**: BYO-01, BYO-02, BYO-03, BYO-04, BYO-05, BYO-06, BYO-07
    75→**Success Criteria** (what must be TRUE):
    76→
    77→1. A user can enter their IPFS node endpoint and credentials in Settings, test the connection, and see a success/failure result
    78→2. After configuring a BYO node, every file upload is pinned to both the CipherBox node (always) and the user's node (best-effort mirror)
    79→3. All IPNS publishes still route through the CipherBox API regardless of BYO configuration (no bypass of optimistic concurrency)
    80→4. BYO users see an advisory quota display (not enforced) with clear indication that storage is managed by their own node
    81→5. The connection test endpoint validates reachability and API compatibility of the user's node before saving configuration
    82→   **Plans**: TBD
    83→
    84→### Phase 22: Performance Baselines Completion
    85→
    86→**Goal**: Complete performance picture exists -- client-side timing, end-to-end journeys, load test results, and capacity recommendations are documented
    87→**Depends on**: Phase 21 (all features must be stable to produce meaningful baselines)
    88→**Requirements**: PERF-05, PERF-06, PERF-07, PERF-08
    89→**Success Criteria** (what must be TRUE):
    90→
    91→1. Client-side timing instrumentation captures encrypt/decrypt throughput, upload/download duration, and IPNS operation timing in the browser
    92→2. End-to-end user journey timings are captured for login-to-vault, upload-to-visible, and share-to-accessible workflows
    93→3. k6 load test scripts simulate concurrent users performing upload, download, publish, and resolve operations with documented pass/fail thresholds
    94→4. Capacity thresholds are documented with scaling recommendations (max concurrent users, storage growth projections, IPNS publish throughput limits)
    95→   **Plans**: TBD
    96→
    97→## Progress
    98→
    99→**Execution Order:**
   100→Phases execute in numeric order: 18 -> 19 -> 20 -> 21 -> 22
   101→
   102→| Phase                              | Milestone | Plans Complete | Status      | Completed |
   103→| ---------------------------------- | --------- | -------------- | ----------- | --------- |
   104→| 18. Performance Instrumentation    | v1.1      | 0/?            | Not started | -         |
   105→| 19. IPNS Resolution Improvement    | v1.1      | 0/?            | Not started | -         |
   106→| 20. Vault Migration                | v1.1      | 0/?            | Not started | -         |
   107→| 21. BYO-IPFS Node Support          | v1.1      | 0/?            | Not started | -         |
   108→| 22. Performance Baselines Complete | v1.1      | 0/?            | Not started | -         |

     1→# Pitfalls Research
     2→
     3→**Domain:** IPFS infrastructure improvements -- replacing delegated routing, migrating DB state to IPFS/IPNS, BYO-IPFS node support, and performance baselines for an existing zero-knowledge encrypted storage app
     4→**Researched:** 2026-03-07
     5→**Confidence:** HIGH for routing/migration pitfalls (verified against codebase + IPFS docs), MEDIUM for BYO-IPFS (fewer production precedents), MEDIUM for instrumentation (well-understood domain but CipherBox-specific interactions need validation)
     6→
     7→**Context:** CipherBox v1.0 shipped 2026-03-05 with 423K lines of TypeScript + Rust across 698 source files. IPNS is already deeply integrated: the `folder_ipns` table tracks per-folder and per-file IPNS records with sequence numbers for optimistic concurrency, the `ipns_republish_schedule` table drives TEE republishing every 6 hours, and `delegated-ipfs.dev` is the sole external routing provider (known unreliable, with DB-cached CID fallback). The database stores auth, vault keys, shares, device approvals, pinned CIDs for quota tracking, and all IPNS state. The recovery tool (`recovery.html`) directly resolves IPNS via `delegated-ipfs.dev` without any API intermediary.
     8→

     1→# Requirements: CipherBox v1.1 IPFS Infrastructure
     2→
     3→**Defined:** 2026-03-07
     4→**Core Value:** Zero-knowledge privacy -- files encrypted client-side, server never sees plaintext
     5→
     6→## v1.1 Requirements
     7→
     8→Requirements for IPFS infrastructure milestone. Each maps to roadmap phases.
     9→
    10→### IPNS Reliability
    11→
    12→- [ ] **IPNS-01**: Self-hosted Someguy deployed alongside Kubo, replacing delegated-ipfs.dev as primary IPNS routing provider
    13→- [ ] **IPNS-02**: IPNS resolution uses DB-first strategy with async Kubo DHT verification via self-hosted Someguy
    14→- [ ] **IPNS-03**: Recovery tool resolves IPNS via self-hosted Someguy instead of delegated-ipfs.dev
    15→- [ ] **IPNS-04**: System degrades gracefully when DHT resolution is slow (timeout + DB fallback within 2s)
    16→
    17→### Vault Migration
    18→
    19→- [ ] **VAULT-01**: rootFolderKey embedded in IPFS vault blob v2 format (ECIES-wrapped in blob header)
    20→- [ ] **VAULT-02**: Client reads rootFolderKey from IPFS blob on login, falls back to DB vaults table
    21→- [ ] **VAULT-03**: Lazy migration writes vault blob v2 on next folder metadata publish
    22→- [ ] **VAULT-04**: encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey column deprecated from vaults table (HKDF-derivable)
    23→- [ ] **VAULT-05**: Recovery tool updated to parse vault blob v2 format
    24→- [ ] **VAULT-06**: Desktop app (Rust) parses vault blob v2 format
    25→
    26→### BYO-IPFS
    27→
    28→- [ ] **BYO-01**: RemotePinningProvider implements standard IPFS Pinning Service API (pin/unpin/status)
    29→- [ ] **BYO-02**: DualPinProvider pins to both CipherBox node and user's configured node
    30→- [ ] **BYO-03**: Per-user IPFS config stored server-side (endpoint URL, encrypted auth token, provider type)
    31→- [ ] **BYO-04**: Settings UI for configuring custom IPFS node endpoint and credentials
    32→- [ ] **BYO-05**: Connection test endpoint validates user's IPFS node is reachable and API-compatible
    33→- [ ] **BYO-06**: All IPNS publishes still route through CipherBox API regardless of BYO config
    34→- [ ] **BYO-07**: Quota tracking becomes advisory for BYO users with clear UI indication
    35→
    36→### Performance Baselines
    37→
    38→- [ ] **PERF-01**: IPFS/IPNS duration histograms added to Prometheus (publish, resolve, pin, cat)
    39→- [ ] **PERF-02**: API endpoint p50/p95/p99 baselines defined per critical route
    40→- [ ] **PERF-03**: Kubo Prometheus endpoint scraped for node health metrics (peers, bandwidth, datastore)
    41→- [ ] **PERF-04**: TEE republish batch duration histogram added
    42→- [ ] **PERF-05**: Client-side timing instrumentation for encrypt/decrypt, upload/download, IPNS operations
    43→- [ ] **PERF-06**: End-to-end user journey timing captured (login-to-vault, upload-to-visible, share-to-accessible)
    44→- [ ] **PERF-07**: k6 load testing scripts simulating concurrent users (upload, download, publish, resolve)
    45→- [ ] **PERF-08**: Capacity thresholds documented with scaling recommendations
    46→
    47→## v1.2 Requirements
    48→
    49→Deferred to future release. Tracked but not in current roadmap.
    50→
    51→### IPNS Enhancements
    52→
    53→- **IPNS-05**: CRDT-based share discovery via IPNS inbox (replace server-side shares table)
    54→- **IPNS-06**: folder_ipns CID cache made advisory (IPNS becomes primary source)
    55→
    56→### Database Minimization
    57→
    58→- **DB-01**: Device registry approval workflow migrated off database
    59→- **DB-02**: pinned_cids table eliminated (alternative quota tracking via IPFS MFS or client-reported)
    60→
    61→### BYO-IPFS Advanced
    62→
    63→- **BYO-08**: Client-direct IPFS upload mode (bypass server relay for power users)
    64→
    65→## Out of Scope
    66→
    67→Explicitly excluded. Documented to prevent scope creep.
    68→
    69→| Feature                             | Reason                                                                                                                                |
    70→| ----------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
    71→| Full database elimination (zero DB) | Auth tables (users, auth_methods, refresh_tokens) require indexed queryable storage. IPFS is not a database.                          |
    72→| IPNS PubSub as primary resolution   | Only works when publisher and resolver share PubSub peers. Not persistent. Doesn't scale to thousands of IPNS names per user.         |
    73→| CRDT for all metadata               | Premature -- optimistic concurrency already solves folder conflicts. CRDTs add state size growth and cross-platform merge complexity. |
    74→| DNSLink as IPNS alternative         | Requires DNS infrastructure per user. Propagation is slow. Doesn't support per-folder/per-file IPNS model.                            |
    75→| Share migration to IPFS             | Requires CRDT inbox protocol (research-only this milestone). Complex query patterns (filter by recipient, status, revocation).        |
    76→| Encrypted Productivity Suite        | Deferred to Milestone 4 (v2.0) -- billing, teams, doc editors, signing, AWS Nitro TEE                                                 |
    77→| Mobile apps                         | Deferred to Milestone 4+                                                                                                              |
    78→
    79→## Traceability
    80→
    81→Which phases cover which requirements. Updated during roadmap creation.
    82→
    83→| Requirement | Phase    | Status  |
    84→| ----------- | -------- | ------- |
    85→| IPNS-01     | Phase 19 | Pending |

=== MATCH ===
Based on the research, here are the features organized by category. Let me scope each one with you.

## IPNS Reliability
**Table stakes:** Self-hosted Someguy (replaces delegated-ipfs.dev), DB-first resolution with async DHT verification, graceful degradation with timeout+fallback
**Differentiators:** Make `folder_ipns` CID cache advisory (not authoritative)
**Research notes:** Someguy is the same software behind delegated-ipfs.dev. Self-hosting it is a Docker Compose addition. ProbeLab data shows 0.3-0.4s DHT median.

## Database Minimization
**Table stakes:** Deprecate `encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey` (already HKDF-derivable, redundant)
**Differentiators:** Move rootFolderKey to IPFS vault blob v2 (server stores zero crypto material)
**Research notes:** Research unanimously flags this as highest-value ZK improvement, but also highest risk — IPNS becomes login-critical. DB copy kept as permanent fallback.

## BYO-IPFS
**Differentiators:** Pinning Service API provider, dual-pin strategy (CipherBox + user node), per-user config + Settings UI, connection test endpoint
**Research notes:** No encrypted storage competitor offers this. Server-relay mode preserves quota tracking and conflict detection. IPNS publishes still go through API.

## Performance Baselines
**Table stakes:** IPFS/IPNS duration histograms, API p50/p95/p99 baselines, Kubo Prometheus scraping
**Differentiators:** End-to-end user journey timing (login-to-vault, upload-to-visible), client-side encryption throughput
...

Let me dig into how `folder_ipns` is used today so I can explain the implications clearly.
...

=== MATCH ===
Here's the breakdown of `folder_ipns` and what "advisory" means:

### What `folder_ipns` does today (6 roles)

1. **CID cache** — `latestCid` stores the most recent CID for each IPNS name. When delegated-ipfs.dev fails (502) or returns stale data, the DB is the fallback.

2. **Sequence number tracking** — `sequenceNumber` is the authoritative counter for optimistic concurrency. On every publish, the API checks `expectedSequenceNumber` against the DB value and returns 409 Conflict on mismatch. This prevents two devices from overwriting each other.

3. **TEE key storage** — `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` + `keyEpoch` hold the IPNS private key wrapped with the TEE public key. The TEE batch republisher reads these to re-sign and republish records every 3-6 hours so they don't expire from the DHT.

4. **TEE republish scheduling** — The republish service queries `folder_ipns` to find all records that need republishing.

5. **User folder enumeration** — `getAllFolderIpns(userId)` lists all a user's IPNS names.

6. **Resolve fallback** — `resolveRecord()` always checks both the network AND the DB, preferring whichever has the higher sequence number (`ipns.service.ts:326-336`).

### What "make it advisory" would mean

The idea is: instead of `folder_ipns` being the **authoritative** source for CIDs and sequence numbers, IPNS resolution (via self-hosted Someguy) becomes primary, and the DB becomes a **performance cache** that's nice to have but not required.

**What would change:**
- Resolve would try Someguy/Kubo DHT first, fall back to DB only on failure
- Sequence number could be read from the IPNS record itself rather than the DB
- Theoretically, a user with BYO-IPFS + reliable IPNS wouldn't need the DB rows at all

**What CANNOT change (blocking dependencies):**
- **TEE republishing** — The TEE needs `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` from this table. No alternative storage exists server-side. This column must stay.
- **Optimistic concurrency** — The 409 conflict check compares sequence numbers atomically in PostgreSQL. Moving this to "resolve IPNS first, then compare" introduces race conditions (two clients resolve the same stale value, both think they're current). The DB check is inherently stronger.
- **Republish scheduling** — The republish cron queries this table to find what needs republishing.

### My assessment

Making `folder_ipns` truly "advisory" is mostly blocked by TEE republishing and concurrency checking — both of which fundamentally need server-side state. You'd be investing effort into a change that can't fully deliver because 2 of the 3 critical columns (`encryptedIpnsPrivateKey`, `sequenceNumber`) must remain authoritative.

The practical gain is small: `latestCid` already works as a "best-of-both" cache (line 326-336 picks the fresher source). Switching from "DB fallback" to "IPNS primary" is essentially just reordering the resolution chain, which the Someguy work already improves.

**Recommendation:** Skip this for v1.1. The Someguy self-hostin
...

## Proposed Roadmap

**5 phases** | **25 requirements mapped** | All covered ✓

| # | Phase | Goal | Requirements | Success Criteria |
|---|-------|------|--------------|------------------|
| 18 | Performance Instrumentation | Observe IPFS/IPNS latency before changes | PERF-01..04 | 4 |
| 19 | IPNS Resolution Improvement | Reliable <2s resolution, kill delegated-ipfs.dev | IPNS-01..04 | 4 |
| 20 | Vault Migration | Server stores zero crypto material | VAULT-01..06 | 5 |
| 21 | BYO-IPFS Node Support | User-configurable pinning with dual-pin | BYO-01..07 | 5 |
| 22 | Performance Baselines Completion | Client timing, load tests, capacity docs | PERF-05..08 | 4 |

**Dependencies:** 18 → 19 → 20 → 21 → 22 (linear)

**Key ordering rationale:**
- Phase 18 first: establish "before" numbers, zero risk
- Phase 19 before 20: IPNS must be reliable before rootFolderKey migration makes it login-critical
- Phase 22 last: needs all features stable for meaningful baselines
...

## IPNS Reliability

=== ASSISTANT (line 889) ===
Here's the breakdown of `folder_ipns` and what "advisory" means:

### What `folder_ipns` does today (6 roles)

1. **CID cache** — `latestCid` stores the most recent CID for each IPNS name. When delegated-ipfs.dev fails (502) or returns stale data, the DB is the fallback.

2. **Sequence number tracking** — `sequenceNumber` is the authoritative counter for optimistic concurrency. On every publish, the API checks `expectedSequenceNumber` against the DB value and returns 409 Conflict on mismatch. This prevents two devices from overwriting each other.

3. **TEE key storage** — `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` + `keyEpoch` hold the IPNS private key wrapped with the TEE public key. The TEE batch republisher reads these to re-sign and republish records every 3-6 hours so they don't expire from the DHT.

4. **TEE republish scheduling** — The republish service queries `folder_ipns` to find all records that need republishing.

5. **User folder enumeration** — `getAllFolderIpns(userId)` lists all a user's IPNS names.

6. **Resolve fallback** — `resolveRecord()` always checks both the network AND the DB, preferring whichever has the higher sequence number (`ipns.service.ts:326-336`).

### What "make it advisory" would mean

The idea is: instead of `folder_ipns` being the **authoritative** source for CIDs and sequence numbers, IPNS resolution (via self-hosted Someguy) becomes primary, and the DB becomes a **performance cache** that's nice to have but not required.

**What would change:**
- Resolve would try Someguy/Kubo DHT first, fall back to DB only on failure
- Sequence number could be read from the IPNS record itself rather than the DB
- Theoretically, a user with BYO-IPFS + reliable IPNS wouldn't need the DB rows at all

**What CANNOT change (blocking dependencies):**
- **TEE republishing** — The TEE needs `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` from this table. No alternative storage exists server-side. This column must stay.
- **Optimistic concurrency** — The 409 conflict check compares sequence numbers atomically in PostgreSQL. Moving this to "resolve IPNS first, then compare" introduces race conditions (two clients resolve the same stale value, both think they're current). The DB check is inherently stronger.
- **Republish scheduling** — The republish cron queries this table to find what needs republishing.

### My assessment

Making `folder_ipns` truly "advisory" is mostly blocked by TEE republishing and concurrency checking — both of which fundamentally need server-side state. You'd be investing effort into a change that can't fully deliver because 2 of the 3 critical columns (`encryptedIpnsPrivateKey`, `sequenceNumber`) must remain authoritative.

The practical gain is small: `latestCid` already works as a "best-of-both" cache (line 326-336 picks the fresher source). Switching from "DB fallback" to "IPNS primary" is essentially just reordering the resolution chain, which the Someguy work already improves.

**Recommendation:** Skip this for v1.1. The Someguy self-hosting already improves IPNS reliability enough that the DB fallback triggers less often. The table stays as-is — it's pulling its weight across all 6 roles.

Want me to re-present the scoping questions now?

## Milestone v1.1 Requirements

### IPNS Reliability
- [ ] **IPNS-01**: Self-hosted Someguy deployed alongside Kubo, replacing delegated-ipfs.dev as primary IPNS routing provider
- [ ] **IPNS-02**: IPNS resolution uses DB-first strategy with async Kubo DHT verification via self-hosted Someguy
- [ ] **IPNS-03**: Recovery tool resolves IPNS via self-hosted Someguy instead of delegated-ipfs.dev
- [ ] **IPNS-04**: System degrades gracefully when DHT resolution is slow (timeout + DB fallback within 2s)

### Vault Migration
- [ ] **VAULT-01**: rootFolderKey embedded in IPFS vault blob v2 format (ECIES-wrapped in blob header)
- [ ] **VAULT-02**: Client reads rootFolderKey from IPFS blob on login, falls back to DB vaults table
- [ ] **VAULT-03**: Lazy migration writes vault blob v2 on next folder metadata publish
- [ ] **VAULT-04**: encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey column deprecated from vaults table (HKDF-derivable)
- [ ] **VAULT-05**: Recovery tool updated to parse vault blob v2 format
- [ ] **VAULT-06**: Desktop app (Rust) parses vault blob v2 format

### BYO-IPFS
- [ ] **BYO-01**: RemotePinningProvider implements standard IPFS Pinning Service API (pin/unpin/status)
- [ ] **BYO-02**: DualPinProvider pins to both CipherBox node and user's configured node
- [ ] **BYO-03**: Per-user IPFS config stored server-side (endpoint URL, encrypted auth token, provider type)
- [ ] **BYO-04**: Settings UI for configuring custom IPFS node endpoint and credentials
- [ ] **BYO-05**: Connection test endpoint validates user's IPFS node is reachable and API-compatible
- [ ] **BYO-06**: All IPNS publishes still route through CipherBox API regardless of BYO config
- [ ] **BYO-07**: Quota tracking becomes advisory for BYO users with clear UI indication

### Performance Baselines
- [ ] **PERF-01**: IPFS/IPNS duration histograms added to Prometheus (publish, resolve, pin, cat)
- [ ] **PERF-02**: API endpoint p50/p95/p99 baselines defined per critical route
- [ ] **PERF-03**: Kubo Prometheus endpoint scraped for node health metrics (peers, bandwidth, datastore)
- [ ] **PERF-04**: TEE republish batch duration histogram added
- [ ] **PERF-05**: Client-side timing instrumentation for encrypt/decrypt, upload/download, IPNS operations
- [ ] **PERF-06**: End-to-end user journey timing captured (login-to-vault, upload-to-visible, share-to-accessible)
- [ ] **PERF-07**: k6 load testing scripts simulating concurrent users (upload, download, publish, resolve)
- [ ] **PERF-08**: Capacity thresholds documented with scaling recommendations

Does this capture what you're building? (yes / adjust)

{
  "file_path": "/Users/michael/Code/cipher-box/.planning/SCOPING_RATIONALE.md",
  "content": "# Scoping Rationale: CipherBox v1.1 IPFS Infrastructure\n\n**Documented:** 2026-03-07\n**Context:** Captures key decisions, tradeoffs, and rejected alternatives from the v1.1 scoping discussion.\n\n---\n\n## Milestone Vision\n\nThe original goal was \"make the database auth-only\" — move everything possible from PostgreSQL to IPFS/IPNS so the server becomes a pure relay. The scoping discussion revealed this is **aspirational for v1.1 but not fully achievable** due to hard dependencies on server-side state.\n\n**Realistic v1.1 outcome:** Migrate vault crypto material off DB, improve IPNS reliability via self-hosting, add BYO-IPFS for data sovereignty, and establish performance baselines. The database remains authoritative for auth, conflict detection, TEE key storage, and share management.\n\n---\n\n## Decision 1: `folder_ipns` Stays Authoritative (Not Advisory)\n\n### What was considered\n\nMaking `folder_ipns` \"advisory\" — IPNS resolution via self-hosted Someguy becomes the primary source of truth, with the DB demoted to a performance cache.\n\n### What `folder_ipns` does today (6 roles)\n\n1. **CID cache** — `latestCid` stores the most recent CID per IPNS name. Fallback when network resolution fails.\n2. **Sequence number tracking** — `sequenceNumber` is the authoritative counter for optimistic concurrency. The API checks `expectedSequenceNumber` against the DB value and returns 409 Conflict on mismatch.\n3. **TEE key storage** — `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` + `keyEpoch` hold IPNS private keys wrapped with TEE public key for republishing.\n4. **TEE republish scheduling** — The republish cron queries `folder_ipns` to find records needing republishing.\n5. **User folder enumeration** — `getAllFolderIpns(userId)` lists all IPNS names for a user.\n6. **Resolve fallback** — `resolveRecord()` checks both network AND DB, preferring whichever has the higher sequence number (`ipns.service.ts:326-336`).\n\n### Why \"advisory\" was rejected\n\nThree of the six roles have **hard blocking dependencies** on server-side state:\n\n- **TEE republishing** needs `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` from this table. No alternative server-side storage exists. This column must stay.\n- **Optimistic concurrency** requires atomic sequence number comparison in PostgreSQL. Moving to \"resolve IPNS first, then compare\" introduces race conditions — two clients resolve the same stale value, both think they're current. The DB check is inherently stronger.\n- **Republish scheduling** queries this table to find what needs republishing.\n\nThe practical gain would be small: `latestCid` already works as a \"best-of-both\" cache (picks the fresher source). Switching from \"DB fallback\" to \"IPNS primary\" is essentially reordering the resolution chain, which the Someguy self-hosting already improves.\n\n### Decision\n\nSkip advisory `folder_ipns` for v1.1. Deferred as IPNS-06 to v1.2 requirements. The Someguy self-hosting improves IPNS reliability enough that the DB fallback triggers less often. The table stays as-is — it's pulling its weight across all 6 roles.\n\n---\n\n## Decision 2: rootFolderKey Migration to IPFS (Accepted with Permanent DB Fallback)\n\n### Tradeoff\n\nMoving `encryptedRootFolderKey` from the `vaults` table into an IPFS vault blob v2 achieves true zero-knowledge (server stores zero crypto material). However, this makes IPNS resolution a login-critical dependency.\n\n### Mitigation\n\n- DB copy of `encryptedRootFolderKey` retained as a **permanent fallback** — not removed after migration\n- Phase 19 (IPNS reliability) is a prerequisite: IPNS must be reliable before rootFolderKey migration makes it login-adjacent\n- Lazy migration on next folder metadata publish — no flag day, no forced re-login\n\n### Why `encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey` is deprecated separately\n\nThis column is **already redundant** — the root IPNS private key is deterministically derivable via HKDF from the user's secp256k1 key. Deprecating it is a cleanup, not a migration.\n\n---\n\n## Decision 3: BYO-IPFS Uses Server-Relay (Not Client-Direct)\n\n### Tradeoff\n\nClient-direct uploads to user's IPFS node would be simpler and reduce server load, but:\n\n- CORS/connectivity issues with arbitrary IPFS endpoints\n- Server-side quota tracking breaks (can't observe what wasn't relayed)\n- **Optimistic concurrency breaks** — IPNS publishes must flow through the API for sequence number checks (BYO-06)\n\n### Decision\n\nServer-relay mode for v1.1. Client-direct deferred as BYO-08 to v1.2 for power users willing to accept the tradeoffs.\n\n---\n\n## Decision 4: Performance Baselines Split Across Two Phases\n\n### Rationale\n\nUser requested client-side instrumentation and load testing scripts to understand scaling thresholds — not just server metrics.\n\nPhase 18 (server-side Prometheus, before any changes) establishes the \"before\" picture. Phase 22 (client-side timing, k6 load tests, capacity docs) comes last because all features must be stable for meaningful baselines. This sandwiching ensures we can measure the impact of phases 19-21.\n\n---\n\n## Decision 5: Shares/Device Approvals Stay in PostgreSQL\n\n### Why not IPFS\n\n- **Shares** require query patterns incompatible with IPFS: filter by recipient, by status, by revocation state. Would need a CRDT inbox protocol.\n- **Device approvals** are inherently transactional (approve/reject workflow with server-side validation).\n- **Republish schedule** is a server-side cron concern.\n\n### Deferred research\n\nCRDT-based share discovery via IPNS inbox is tracked as a v1.2 research item (IPNS-05). See `todos/pending/2026-02-22-crdt-ipns-inbox-sharing.md`.\n\n---\n\n## Decision 6: IPNS PubSub and DNSLink Rejected\n\n| Alternative | Why rejected |\n|---|---|\n| **IPNS over PubSub** | Only works when publisher and resolver share PubSub peers. Not persistent. Doesn't scale to thousands of IPNS names per user. |\n| **DNSLink** | Requires DNS infrastructure per user. Propagation is slow. Incompatible with per-folder/per-file IPNS model. |\n\n---\n\n## Tables Not Migrated (v1.1)\n\nFor reference, these PostgreSQL tables remain and why:\n\n| Table | Stays because |\n|---|---|\n| `users` | Auth — indexed lookups by hashed identifier |\n| `auth_methods` | Auth — MFA factor storage |\n| `refresh_tokens` | Auth — session management |\n| `folder_ipns` | TEE keys, sequence numbers, republish scheduling (see Decision 1) |\n| `ipns_republish_schedule` | Server-side cron scheduling |\n| `shares` / `share_keys` | Query-heavy, needs CRDT inbox for IPFS (see Decision 5) |\n| `device_approvals` | Transactional approve/reject workflow |\n| `pinned_cids` | Quota tracking (alternative needed before removal) |\n\n---\n\n_Documented from v1.1 scoping session, 2026-03-07_\n"
}

**Stack additions:** Zero new npm dependencies. Kubo RPC for IPNS resolution, existing `prom-client` for histograms, IPFS Pinning Service API for BYO-IPFS (native fetch, no SDK).

**Feature table stakes:** DB-first IPNS resolution (replace delegated-ipfs.dev), vault blob v2 (rootFolderKey to IPFS), dual-pin BYO-IPFS with Pinning Service API, Prometheus histograms for latency baselines.

**Watch Out For:** IPNS DHT records expire in 48 hours — migration gap can cause record loss. rootFolderKey is the only non-derivable key — keep DB copy as fallback during migration. BYO-IPFS must still flow publishes through API for sequence number checks. Shares/share_keys CANNOT migrate to IPFS without CRDT inbox (research-only this milestone).

**Key scoping insight:** "Database to auth-only" is aspirational for v1.1. Realistically: migrate vault crypto material off DB, make folder_ipns advisory, but shares/device_approvals/republish_schedule must stay in PostgreSQL.

=== ASSISTANT (line 863) ===
Based on the research, here are the features organized by category. Let me scope each one with you.

## IPNS Reliability
**Table stakes:** Self-hosted Someguy (replaces delegated-ipfs.dev), DB-first resolution with async DHT verification, graceful degradation with timeout+fallback
**Differentiators:** Make `folder_ipns` CID cache advisory (not authoritative)
**Research notes:** Someguy is the same software behind delegated-ipfs.dev. Self-hosting it is a Docker Compose addition. ProbeLab data shows 0.3-0.4s DHT median.

## Database Minimization
**Table stakes:** Deprecate `encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey` (already HKDF-derivable, redundant)
**Differentiators:** Move rootFolderKey to IPFS vault blob v2 (server stores zero crypto material)
**Research notes:** Research unanimously flags this as highest-value ZK improvement, but also highest risk — IPNS becomes login-critical. DB copy kept as permanent fallback.

## BYO-IPFS
**Differentiators:** Pinning Service API provider, dual-pin strategy (CipherBox + user node), per-user config + Settings UI, connection test endpoint
**Research notes:** No encrypted storage competitor offers this. Server-relay mode preserves quota tracking and conflict detection. IPNS publishes still go through API.

## Performance Baselines
**Table stakes:** IPFS/IPNS duration histograms, API p50/p95/p99 baselines, Kubo Prometheus scraping
**Differentiators:** End-to-end user journey timing (login-to-vault, upload-to-visible), client-side encryption throughput


=== ASSISTANT (line 889) ===
Here's the breakdown of `folder_ipns` and what "advisory" means:

### What `folder_ipns` does today (6 roles)

1. **CID cache** — `latestCid` stores the most recent CID for each IPNS name. When delegated-ipfs.dev fails (502) or returns stale data, the DB is the fallback.

2. **Sequence number tracking** — `sequenceNumber` is the authoritative counter for optimistic concurrency. On every publish, the API checks `expectedSequenceNumber` against the DB value and returns 409 Conflict on mismatch. This prevents two devices from overwriting each other.

3. **TEE key storage** — `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` + `keyEpoch` hold the IPNS private key wrapped with the TEE public key. The TEE batch republisher reads these to re-sign and republish records every 3-6 hours so they don't expire from the DHT.

4. **TEE republish scheduling** — The republish service queries `folder_ipns` to find all records that need republishing.

5. **User folder enumeration** — `getAllFolderIpns(userId)` lists all a user's IPNS names.

6. **Resolve fallback** — `resolveRecord()` always checks both the network AND the DB, preferring whichever has the higher sequence number (`ipns.service.ts:326-336`).

### What "make it advisory" would mean

The idea is: instead of `folder_ipns` being the **authoritative** source for CIDs and sequence numbers, IPNS resolution (via self-hosted Someguy) becomes primary, and the DB becomes a **performance cache** that's nice to have but not required.

**What would change:**
- Resolve would try Someguy/Kubo DHT first, fall back to DB only on failure
- Sequence number could be read from the IPNS record itself rather than the DB
- Theoretically, a user with BYO-IPFS + reliable IPNS wouldn't need the DB rows at all

**What CANNOT change (blocking dependencies):**
- **TEE republishing** — The TEE needs `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` from this table. No alternative storage exists server-side. This column must stay.
- **Optimistic concurrency** — The 409 conflict check compares sequence numbers atomically in PostgreSQL. Moving this to "resolve IPNS first, then compare" introduces race conditions (two clients resolve the same stale value, both think they're current). The DB check is inherently stronger.
- **Republish scheduling** — The republish cron queries this table to find what needs republishing.

### My assessment

Making `folder_ipns` truly "advisory" is mostly blocked by TEE republishing and concurrency checking — both of which fundamentally need server-side state. You'd be investing effort into a change that can't fully deliver because 2 of the 3 critical columns (`encryptedIpnsPrivateKey`, `sequenceNumber`) must remain authoritative.

The practical gain is small: `latestCid` already works as a "best-of-both" cache (line 326-336 picks the fresher source). Switching from "DB fallback" to "IPNS primary" is essentially just reordering the resolution chain, which the Someguy work already improves.

**Recommendation:** Skip this for v1.1. The Someguy self-hosting already improves IPNS reliability enough that the DB fallback triggers less often. The table stays as-is — it's pulling its weight across all 6 roles.

Want me to re-present the scoping questions now?

## Milestone v1.1 Requirements

### IPNS Reliability
- [ ] **IPNS-01**: Self-hosted Someguy deployed alongside Kubo, replacing delegated-ipfs.dev as primary IPNS routing provider
- [ ] **IPNS-02**: IPNS resolution uses DB-first strategy with async Kubo DHT verification via self-hosted Someguy
- [ ] **IPNS-03**: Recovery tool resolves IPNS via self-hosted Someguy instead of delegated-ipfs.dev
- [ ] **IPNS-04**: System degrades gracefully when DHT resolution is slow (timeout + DB fallback within 2s)

### Vault Migration
- [ ] **VAULT-01**: rootFolderKey embedded in IPFS vault blob v2 format (ECIES-wrapped in blob header)
- [ ] **VAULT-02**: Client reads rootFolderKey from IPFS blob on login, falls back to DB vaults table
- [ ] **VAULT-03**: Lazy migration writes vault blob v2 on next folder metadata publish
- [ ] **VAULT-04**: encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey column deprecated from vaults table (HKDF-derivable)
- [ ] **VAULT-05**: Recovery tool updated to parse vault blob v2 format
- [ ] **VAULT-06**: Desktop app (Rust) parses vault blob v2 format

### BYO-IPFS
- [ ] **BYO-01**: RemotePinningProvider implements standard IPFS Pinning Service API (pin/unpin/status)
- [ ] **BYO-02**: DualPinProvider pins to both CipherBox node and user's configured node
- [ ] **BYO-03**: Per-user IPFS config stored server-side (endpoint URL, encrypted auth token, provider type)
- [ ] **BYO-04**: Settings UI for configuring custom IPFS node endpoint and credentials
- [ ] **BYO-05**: Connection test endpoint validates user's IPFS node is reachable and API-compatible
- [ ] **BYO-06**: All IPNS publishes still route through CipherBox API regardless of BYO config
- [ ] **BYO-07**: Quota tracking becomes advisory for BYO users with clear UI indication

### Performance Baselines
- [ ] **PERF-01**: IPFS/IPNS duration histograms added to Prometheus (publish, resolve, pin, cat)
- [ ] **PERF-02**: API endpoint p50/p95/p99 baselines defined per critical route
- [ ] **PERF-03**: Kubo Prometheus endpoint scraped for node health metrics (peers, bandwidth, datastore)
- [ ] **PERF-04**: TEE republish batch duration histogram added
- [ ] **PERF-05**: Client-side timing instrumentation for encrypt/decrypt, upload/download, IPNS operations
- [ ] **PERF-06**: End-to-end user journey timing captured (login-to-vault, upload-to-visible, share-to-accessible)
- [ ] **PERF-07**: k6 load testing scripts simulating concurrent users (upload, download, publish, resolve)
- [ ] **PERF-08**: Capacity thresholds documented with scaling recommendations

--- ASSISTANT (line 866) ---
What would you like to clarify before we scope these?

--- ASSISTANT (line 873) ---
Let me dig into how `folder_ipns` is used today so I can explain the implications clearly.

--- ASSISTANT (line 889) ---
Here's the breakdown of `folder_ipns` and what "advisory" means:

### What `folder_ipns` does today (6 roles)

1. **CID cache** — `latestCid` stores the most recent CID for each IPNS name. When delegated-ipfs.dev fails (502) or returns stale data, the DB is the fallback.

2. **Sequence number tracking** — `sequenceNumber` is the authoritative counter for optimistic concurrency. On every publish, the API checks `expectedSequenceNumber` against the DB value and returns 409 Conflict on mismatch. This prevents two devices from overwriting each other.

3. **TEE key storage** — `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` + `keyEpoch` hold the IPNS private key wrapped with the TEE public key. The TEE batch republisher reads these to re-sign and republish records every 3-6 hours so they don't expire from the DHT.

4. **TEE republish scheduling** — The republish service queries `folder_ipns` to find all records that need republishing.

5. **User folder enumeration** — `getAllFolderIpns(userId)` lists all a user's IPNS names.

6. **Resolve fallback** — `resolveRecord()` always checks both the network AND the DB, preferring whichever has the higher sequence number (`ipns.service.ts:326-336`).

### What "make it advisory" would mean

The idea is: instead of `folder_ipns` being the **authoritative** source for CIDs and sequence numbers, IPNS resolution (via self-hosted Someguy) becomes primary, and the DB becomes a **performance cache** that's nice to have but not required.

**What would change:**
- Resolve would try Someguy/Kubo DHT first, fall back to DB only on failure
- Sequence number could be read from the IPNS record itself rather than the DB
- Theoretically, a user with BYO-IPFS + reliable IPNS wouldn't need the DB rows at all

**What CANNOT change (blocking dependencies):**
- **TEE republishing** — The TEE needs `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` from this table. No alternative storage exists server-side. This column must stay.
- **Optimistic concurrency** — The 409 conflict check compares sequence numbers atomically in PostgreSQL. Moving this to "resolve IPNS first, then compare" introduces race conditions (two clients resolve the same stale value, both think they're current). The DB check is inherently stronger.
- **Republish scheduling** — The republish cron queries this table to find what needs republishing.

### My assessment

Making `folder_ipns` truly "advisory" is mostly blocked by TEE republishing and concurrency checking — both of which fundamentally need server-side state. You'd be investing effort into a change that can't fully deliver because 2 of the 3 critical columns (`encryptedIpnsPrivateKey`, `sequenceNumber`) must remain authoritative.

The practical gain is small: `latestCid` already works as a "best-of-both" cache (line 326-336 picks the fresher source). Switching from "DB fallback" to "IPNS primary" is essentially just reordering the resolution chain, which the Someguy work already improves.

**Recommendation:** Skip this for v1.1. The Someguy self-hosting already improves IPNS reliability enough that the DB fallback triggers less often. The table stays as-is — it's pulling its weight across all 6 roles.

Want me to re-present the scoping questions now?

> AGENT

Now I see the full picture. The concession is: even though the milestone is called "IPFS Infrastructure" and the vision is IPFS-native, the resolution strategy deliberately keeps DB as **first** in the resolution chain (IPNS-02: "DB-first strategy with async DHT verification"). This is a pragmatic concession — the "IPFS-native" ideal would be DHT-first with DB fallback, but that was rejected because:

1. DHT resolution is slower and less reliable than a local DB read
2. The DB already has the correct CID from the publish path
3. Async DHT verification catches staleness without blocking the user

88:2. **DB cache is already the reliable source.** The `IpnsService.resolveRecord()` method (lines 290-350 of `ipns.service.ts`) already prefers DB cache when its sequence number is higher than the network result. Making DB-first explicit simplifies the architecture.
89-3. **Kubo DHT resolution has improved.** Kubo 0.38+ has sweep providers with 97% fewer DHT lookups for large-scale operations. IPNS TTL defaults dropped from 1 hour to 5 minutes in recent releases, so changes propagate faster.
90-4. **PubSub for same-node resolution.** Kubo supports `Ipns.UsePubsub` for near-instant IPNS record propagation between connected peers. When both the publishing client and the TEE republisher connect through the same Kubo node, PubSub makes resolution instantaneous.
91-

     1→# Scoping Rationale: CipherBox v1.1 IPFS Infrastructure
     2→
     3→**Documented:** 2026-03-07
     4→**Context:** Captures key decisions, tradeoffs, and rejected alternatives from the v1.1 scoping discussion.
     5→
     6→---
     7→
     8→## Milestone Vision
     9→
    10→The original goal was "make the database auth-only" — move everything possible from PostgreSQL to IPFS/IPNS so the server becomes a pure relay. The scoping discussion revealed this is **aspirational for v1.1 but not fully achievable** due to hard dependencies on server-side state.
    11→
    12→**Realistic v1.1 outcome:** Migrate vault crypto material off DB, improve IPNS reliability via self-hosting, add BYO-IPFS for data sovereignty, and establish performance baselines. The database remains authoritative for auth, conflict detection, TEE key storage, and share management.
    13→
    14→---
    15→
    16→## Decision 1: `folder_ipns` Stays Authoritative (Not Advisory)
    17→
    18→### What was considered
    19→
    20→Making `folder_ipns` "advisory" — IPNS resolution via self-hosted Someguy becomes the primary source of truth, with the DB demoted to a performance cache.
    21→
    22→### What `folder_ipns` does today (6 roles)
    23→
    24→1. **CID cache** — `latestCid` stores the most recent CID per IPNS name. Fallback when network resolution fails.
    25→2. **Sequence number tracking** — `sequenceNumber` is the authoritative counter for optimistic concurrency. The API checks `expectedSequenceNumber` against the DB value and returns 409 Conflict on mismatch.
    26→3. **TEE key storage** — `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` + `keyEpoch` hold IPNS private keys wrapped with TEE public key for republishing.
    27→4. **TEE republish scheduling** — The republish cron queries `folder_ipns` to find records needing republishing.
    28→5. **User folder enumeration** — `getAllFolderIpns(userId)` lists all IPNS names for a user.
    29→6. **Resolve fallback** — `resolveRecord()` checks both network AND DB, preferring whichever has the higher sequence number (`ipns.service.ts:326-336`).
    30→
    31→### Why "advisory" was rejected
    32→
    33→Three of the six roles have **hard blocking dependencies** on server-side state:
    34→
    35→- **TEE republishing** needs `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` from this table. No alternative server-side storage exists. This column must stay.
    36→- **Optimistic concurrency** requires atomic sequence number comparison in PostgreSQL. Moving to "resolve IPNS first, then compare" introduces race conditions — two clients resolve the same stale value, both think they're current. The DB check is inherently stronger.
    37→- **Republish scheduling** queries this table to find what needs republishing.
    38→
    39→The practical gain would be small: `latestCid` already works as a "best-of-both" cache (picks the fresher source). Switching from "DB fallback" to "IPNS primary" is essentially reordering the resolution chain, which the Someguy self-hosting already improves.
    40→
    41→### Decision
    42→
    43→Skip advisory `folder_ipns` for v1.1. Deferred as IPNS-06 to v1.2 requirements. The Someguy self-hosting improves IPNS reliability enough that the DB fallback triggers less often. The table stays as-is — it's pulling its weight across all 6 roles.
    44→
    45→---
    46→
    47→## Decision 2: rootFolderKey Migration to IPFS (Accepted with Permanent DB Fallback)
    48→
    49→### Tradeoff
    50→
    51→Moving `encryptedRootFolderKey` from the `vaults` table into an IPFS vault blob v2 achieves true zero-knowledge (server stores zero crypto material). However, this makes IPNS resolution a login-critical dependency.
    52→
    53→### Mitigation
    54→
    55→- DB copy of `encryptedRootFolderKey` retained as a **permanent fallback** — not removed after migration
    56→- Phase 19 (IPNS reliability) is a prerequisite: IPNS must be reliable before rootFolderKey migration makes it login-adjacent
    57→- Lazy migration on next folder metadata publish — no flag day, no forced re-login
    58→
    59→### Why `encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey` is deprecated separately
    60→
    61→This column is **already redundant** — the root IPNS private key is deterministically derivable via HKDF from the user's secp256k1 key. Deprecating it is a cleanup, not a migration.
    62→
    63→---
    64→
    65→## Decision 3: BYO-IPFS Uses Server-Relay (Not Client-Direct)
    66→
    67→### Tradeoff
    68→
    69→Client-direct uploads to user's IPFS node would be simpler and reduce server load, but:
    70→
    71→- CORS/connectivity issues with arbitrary IPFS endpoints
    72→- Server-side quota tracking breaks (can't observe what wasn't relayed)
    73→- **Optimistic concurrency breaks** — IPNS publishes must flow through the API for sequence number checks (BYO-06)
    74→
    75→### Decision
    76→
    77→Server-relay mode for v1.1. Client-direct deferred as BYO-08 to v1.2 for power users willing to accept the tradeoffs.
    78→
    79→---
    80→
    81→## Decision 4: Performance Baselines Split Across Two Phases
    82→
    83→### Rationale
    84→
    85→User requested client-side instrumentation and load testing scripts to understand scaling thresholds — not just server metrics.
    86→
    87→Phase 18 (server-side Prometheus, before any changes) establishes the "before" picture. Phase 22 (client-side timing, k6 load tests, capacity docs) comes last because all features must be stable for meaningful baselines. This sandwiching ensures we can measure the impact of phases 19-21.
    88→
    89→---
    90→
    91→## Decision 5: Shares/Device Approvals Stay in PostgreSQL
    92→
    93→### Why not IPFS
    94→
    95→- **Shares** require query patterns incompatible with IPFS: filter by recipient, by status, by revocation state. Would need a CRDT inbox protocol.
    96→- **Device approvals** are inherently transactional (approve/reject workflow with server-side validation).
    97→- **Republish schedule** is a server-side cron concern.
    98→
    99→### Deferred research
   100→
   101→CRDT-based share discovery via IPNS inbox is tracked as a v1.2 research item (IPNS-05). See `todos/pending/2026-02-22-crdt-ipns-inbox-sharing.md`.
   102→
   103→---
   104→
   105→## Decision 6: IPNS PubSub and DNSLink Rejected
   106→
   107→| Alternative          | Why rejected                                                                                                                  |
   108→| -------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
   109→| **IPNS over PubSub** | Only works when publisher and resolver share PubSub peers. Not persistent. Doesn't scale to thousands of IPNS names per user. |
   110→| **DNSLink**          | Requires DNS infrastructure per user. Propagation is slow. Incompatible with per-folder/per-file IPNS model.                  |
   111→
   112→---
   113→
   114→## Tables Not Migrated (v1.1)
   115→
   116→For reference, these PostgreSQL tables remain and why:
   117→
   118→| Table                     | Stays because                                                     |
   119→| ------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- |
   120→| `users`                   | Auth — indexed lookups by hashed identifier                       |
   121→| `auth_methods`            | Auth — MFA factor storage                                         |
   122→| `refresh_tokens`          | Auth — session management                                         |
   123→| `folder_ipns`             | TEE keys, sequence numbers, republish scheduling (see Decision 1) |
   124→| `ipns_republish_schedule` | Server-side cron scheduling                                       |
   125→| `shares` / `share_keys`   | Query-heavy, needs CRDT inbox for IPFS (see Decision 5)           |
   126→| `device_approvals`        | Transactional approve/reject workflow                             |
   127→| `pinned_cids`             | Quota tracking (alternative needed before removal)                |
   128→
   129→---
   130→
   131→Documented from v1.1 scoping session, 2026-03-07
   132→
```

  "old_string": "## Decision 2: rootFolderKey Migration to IPFS (Accepted with Permanent DB Fallback)",
  "new_string": "## Decision 2: IPNS Resolution Keeps DB-First Strategy (Not DHT-First)\n\n### The concession\n\nA milestone called \"IPFS Infrastructure\" might suggest moving IPNS resolution to be DHT-primary — resolve via self-hosted Someguy/Kubo first, fall back to DB only on failure. The scoping discussion explicitly chose the opposite: **DB-first with async DHT verification**.\n\n### Current flow (before v1.1)\n\n```text\nresolveRecord()\n  -> DelegatedRoutingClient.resolve() -> delegated-ipfs.dev (primary, 10s timeout)\n  -> folder_ipns DB query (fallback on 502/timeout)\n  -> Compare sequence numbers, return highest\n```\n\n### v1.1 flow (IPNS-02)\n\n```text\nresolveRecord()\n  -> folder_ipns DB query (primary, <5ms)\n  -> Return DB result immediately to caller\n  -> Async: Kubo DHT resolve via self-hosted Someguy (background)\n     -> If DHT has higher sequence number, update DB cache\n     -> If DHT fails, no-op (DB is authoritative for our own records)\n```\n\n### Why DB-first instead of DHT-first\n\n1. **Latency:** DB query completes in <5ms. DHT resolution, even self-hosted, has a median of 0.3-0.4s (ProbeLab data) and a long tail. Users should never wait for the DHT on the hot path.\n2. **DB is already correct for our own records.** Every publish writes to `folder_ipns` atomically. The DB always has the latest CID that *we* published. The only scenario where DHT has a fresher value is if another node published for the same IPNS name — which doesn't happen in CipherBox's architecture.\n3. **Graceful degradation is free.** If Someguy/Kubo is down, nothing changes — the DB result is already returned. No timeout, no retry, no user-visible degradation (IPNS-04).\n4. **Async verification catches staleness.** The background DHT check is a consistency audit, not a user-facing operation. If a TEE republish advanced the sequence number and the DB hasn't caught up, the async check self-heals.\n\n### What this means for \"IPFS-native\"\n\nThis is a pragmatic concession: the DB remains the primary source of truth for IPNS resolution, not IPFS. The milestone improves IPNS *infrastructure* (self-hosting, reliability) without making it the *authority*. True DHT-primary resolution is deferred to v1.2 (IPNS-06: folder_ipns CID cache made advisory) and depends on proving Someguy reliability at scale.\n\n---\n\n## Decision 3: rootFolderKey Migration to IPFS (Accepted with Permanent DB Fallback)"
}
```

> AGENT

Added **Decision 2: IPNS Resolution Keeps DB-First Strategy** to the scoping rationale. It documents the concession with:

- Before/after resolution flow diagrams showing the switch from `delegated-ipfs.dev primary → DB fallback` to `DB primary → async DHT verification`
- Four reasons why DB-first was chosen over DHT-first (latency, correctness for self-published records, free graceful degradation, async self-healing)
- Explicit acknowledgment that this is a pragmatic concession from the "IPFS-native" vision — the DB stays authoritative, with DHT-primary deferred to v1.2 (IPNS-06)

   175→**For client publishes:** The API receives pre-signed record bytes via `POST /ipns/publish`. The API:
   176→
   177→1. Upserts the `folder_ipns` DB record (unchanged -- this is the reliable CID source)
   178→2. Broadcasts the pre-signed record to the DHT via Kubo's `/api/v0/routing/put` (replaces delegated-ipfs.dev)
   179→3. Falls back to delegated-ipfs.dev only if Kubo routing put fails and the fallback is enabled
   180→
   181→**For TEE republishes:** The TEE already has the decrypted IPNS private key in hardware. Instead of TEE signing a record and the API broadcasting via delegated routing, the TEE can return the raw Ed25519 private key bytes (still inside the TEE boundary), and the API can:
   182→
   183→1. Import the key temporarily into Kubo's keystore (`/api/v0/key/import`)
   184→2. Call `/api/v0/name/publish` directly through Kubo
   185→3. Remove the key from Kubo's keystore (`/api/v0/key/rm`)
   186→
   187→This bypasses delegated-ipfs.dev entirely for republishing. However, this is an optimization, not a requirement. The existing TEE->signed record->delegated routing path still works as a fallback.
   188→
   189→**Security note on Kubo key import:** The IPNS private key is transiently present in Kubo's memory during the publish call. This is acceptable because:
   190→
   191→- Kubo already runs on the same host as the API in a Docker network
   192→- The key is encrypted at rest in the DB (encrypted with TEE public key)
   193→- The key is removed from Kubo's keystore immediately after publish
   194→- The alternative (delegated-ipfs.dev) sends the signed record over the internet, which is a larger attack surface
   195→
   196→### 2.5 Kubo Configuration Changes
   197→
   198→Enable on the self-hosted Kubo node:
   199→
   200→```json
   201→{
   202→  "Ipns": {
   203→    "UsePubsub": true
   204→  },
```

   335→The server must enforce storage quotas. If quota tracking moves to IPFS, a malicious client could lie about their storage usage. The server must independently track pinned CID sizes.
   336→
   337→### 3.2 Post-Migration Database Role
   338→
   339→After v1.1 migrations, the database serves these purposes:
   340→
   341→| Purpose              | Tables                                                             | Why Server-Side                   |
   342→| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------- |
   343→| Identity             | `users`, `auth_methods`                                            | Server-mediated auth              |
   344→| Sessions             | `refresh_tokens`                                                   | JWT lifecycle                     |
   345→| Quota enforcement    | `pinned_cids`                                                      | Server must prevent quota abuse   |
   346→| Publish coordination | `folder_ipns`                                                      | Sequence numbers for concurrency  |
   347→| TEE orchestration    | `ipns_republish_schedule`, `tee_key_state`, `tee_key_rotation_log` | Server schedules TEE work         |
   348→| Sharing graph        | `shares`, `share_keys`, `share_invites`                            | Until CRDT inbox (v1.2+)          |
   349→| MFA                  | `device_approvals`                                                 | Short-lived approval state        |
   350→| Vault metadata       | `vaults` (reduced)                                                 | ownerPublicKey, rootIpnsName only |
   351→
   352→**What was eliminated:** Server no longer stores any user crypto material (encryptedRootFolderKey, encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey). The server becomes a coordination relay, not a key escrow.
   353→

   355→

    95→---
    96→
    97→## Feature 2: Database Minimization (Migrate State to IPFS/IPNS)
    98→
    99→### Architecture Decision
   100→
   101→Five categories of data currently live in PostgreSQL that should move to IPFS/IPNS:
   102→
   103→| Data                      | Current Location        | Target Location                     | Migration Approach                |
   104→| ------------------------- | ----------------------- | ----------------------------------- | --------------------------------- |
   105→| `encryptedRootFolderKey`  | `vaults` table          | Root vault IPNS blob on IPFS        | New blob format, client migration |
   106→| Share records             | `shares` / `share_keys` | IPNS inbox per recipient (future)   | Defer to CRDT research            |
   107→| Device registry           | N/A (client-side)       | Vault IPNS blob extension           | Metadata schema extension         |
   108→| Folder/file IPNS tracking | `folder_ipns` table     | Derivable from folder metadata tree | Client-side traversal             |
   109→| Pinned CID tracking       | `pinned_cids` table     | Keep in DB (quota enforcement)      | No change                         |
   110→
   111→**Key insight from the todo analysis:** Moving `encryptedRootFolderKey` to IPFS is the highest-value migration. It eliminates all crypto material from the server, making the database purely auth-related. The other migrations are either lower priority (share discovery via CRDT is research-only this milestone per PROJECT.md) or already partially in place (device registry is client-side).
   112→
   113→### Technology Needed
   114→
   115→**None new.** The migration uses:
   116→
   117→- Existing `@cipherbox/crypto` package for metadata schema evolution (add `encryptedRootFolderKey` to the root vault IPNS blob format)
   118→- Existing `IpfsProvider.pinFile()` for writing the new blob
   119→- Existing `IpnsService.publishRecord()` for publishing the updated IPNS record
