   195→}
   196→```
   197→
   198→The `RemotePinningProvider` calls `POST /pins` with the CID after local pinning. It does NOT replace the local provider -- it supplements it. The local Kubo node remains the source of truth for reads.
   199→
   200→### User Settings Storage
   201→
   202→Per-user IPFS configuration stored in the vault settings (encrypted, client-side):
   203→
   204→```typescript
   205→// In vault metadata (IPNS blob, client-encrypted)
   206→interface VaultSettings {
   207→  remotePinning?: {
   208→    enabled: boolean;
   209→    endpoint: string;
   210→    providerName: string;
   211→    // accessToken stored separately in memory, NOT in metadata
   212→  };
   213→}
   214→```
   215→
   216→The access token is stored encrypted in IndexedDB (using existing `idb` from M2) or entered per-session. It is NEVER sent to the CipherBox API -- the remote pinning calls are made by the NestJS backend using a token the user provides via an encrypted channel.
   217→
   218→**Alternative approach considered: Client-direct pinning.** The web app could call the user's pinning endpoint directly. This avoids the server seeing the access token but introduces CORS issues (most pinning services don't set `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` for arbitrary origins) and breaks desktop parity (Tauri apps don't have CORS restrictions but would need a different code path).
   219→
   220→**Recommendation: Server-relay for v1.1.** The server relays pin requests using an access token the user provides encrypted with the server's public key. The server sees the token but never the plaintext content (it only pins CIDs of encrypted blobs). Evaluate client-direct as a privacy upgrade in a future milestone.
   221→
   222→### Quota and Conflict Considerations
   223→
   224→| Concern            | BYO-IPFS Behavior                                             |
   225→| ------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------- |
   226→| Storage quota      | Advisory only. Server can't enforce quotas on user's node.    |
   227→| Conflict detection | Still works -- IPNS publish goes through CipherBox API.       |
   228→| TEE republishing   | Unaffected -- TEE republishes IPNS records, not file content. |
   229→| File availability  | Content available from both local Kubo and user's node.       |
```

   481→## Roadmap Implications for Stack
   482→
   483→### Phase Ordering by Stack Dependency
   484→
   485→1. **IPNS reliability first** -- Enables `Gateway.ExposeRoutingAPI`, points routing at local Kubo. No code dependencies on other features. Unblocks everything else.
   486→
   487→2. **Performance baselines second** -- Add Prometheus histograms before any other changes so you have "before" measurements. k6 scripts establish the baseline numbers.

     1→# Project Research Summary
     2→
     3→**Project:** CipherBox v1.1 -- IPFS Infrastructure
     4→**Domain:** IPFS/IPNS reliability, database minimization, BYO-IPFS node support, performance baselines
     5→**Researched:** 2026-03-07
     6→**Confidence:** HIGH
     7→
     8→## Executive Summary
     9→
    10→CipherBox v1.1 is an infrastructure-hardening milestone, not a feature milestone. The four workstreams -- IPNS reliability, database minimization, BYO-IPFS, and performance baselines -- aim to transform CipherBox from "IPFS as a storage backend with database fallbacks" to "IPFS-native with the database serving only coordination and auth." The dominant finding across all four research files is that the existing stack already has the capabilities needed. The Kubo v0.34.0 node can resolve IPNS records locally via its DHT; the `IpfsProvider` interface already abstracts pin/unpin/get for provider extension; `prom-client` is installed with an established `MetricsService` pattern; and TypeORM handles all necessary migrations. **Zero new npm dependencies are required for this entire milestone.**
    11→
    12→The recommended approach is a four-phase execution with strict ordering. Phase 1 (performance instrumentation) is zero-risk and establishes baselines before any architectural changes. Phase 2 (IPNS reliability) inverts the resolution model to DB-first with async Kubo DHT verification, eliminating the `delegated-ipfs.dev` dependency. Phase 3 (database minimization) moves `encryptedRootFolderKey` into an IPFS blob via a versioned vault blob v2 format, with dual-write migration and DB fallback retained for disaster recovery. Phase 4 (BYO-IPFS) adds a `RemotePinningProvider` speaking the standard IPFS Pinning Service API, with dual-pin strategy (always pin to CipherBox node, best-effort mirror to user's node).
    13→
    14→The primary risk is in Phase 3: moving rootFolderKey to IPFS creates a hard dependency on IPNS resolution for login. All four research files flag this independently. The mitigation is clear -- keep the DB copy as a permanent fallback, never make IPNS the sole access path for the root folder key. Secondary risks include sequence number divergence during routing provider migration (Phase 2) and quota tracking becoming unenforceable for BYO-IPFS users (Phase 4). Both have well-defined prevention strategies documented in PITFALLS.md.
    15→
    16→## Key Findings
    17→
    18→### Recommended Stack
    19→
    20→The milestone requires zero new npm dependencies. Every capability is built using existing libraries, configuration changes, and new provider implementations. The only external tooling addition is Grafana k6 v1.0 (a standalone Go binary, not an npm package) for load testing scripts.
    21→
    22→**Core technologies (all existing):**
    23→
    24→- **Kubo v0.34.0 (recommend upgrade to v0.40.1):** Self-hosted IPFS node already participating in the Amino DHT. Supports `Gateway.ExposeRoutingAPI` and `Ipns.UsePubsub` for local IPNS resolution. v0.40.1 makes routing API default and improves IPNS-over-PubSub.
    25→- **`prom-client` ^15.1.3:** Already installed with `MetricsService` pattern established. Extend with 4 new histograms for IPFS/IPNS latency tracking.
    26→- **TypeORM ^0.3.28 + PostgreSQL 16.x:** Handles all migration needs. 2-3 new migrations (ADD COLUMN, ALTER COLUMN, CREATE TABLE for user IPFS config).
    27→- **IPFS Pinning Service API (HTTP standard):** Vendor-agnostic OpenAPI spec with 4 endpoints. Implemented by Pinata, Filebase, web3.storage, IPFS Cluster. No SDK needed -- native `fetch` is sufficient.
    28→- **k6 v1.0+ (dev tooling only):** Standalone load test runner with native TypeScript support. Install via `brew install k6`. Not committed to repo.
    29→
    30→### Expected Features
    31→
    32→**Must have (table stakes):**
    33→
    34→- Reliable IPNS resolution (<2s, >99.5% availability) -- current `delegated-ipfs.dev` has documented 502s and stale records
    35→- DB-cached CID fallback with sequence number comparison -- already implemented, needs to become the primary resolution path
    36→- IPNS publish/resolve latency monitoring -- counters exist, need duration histograms
    37→- API endpoint response time baselines with p50/p95/p99 targets
    38→- Graceful degradation when IPFS/IPNS is slow -- timeout + fallback pattern, partially implemented
    39→
    40→**Should have (differentiators):**
    41→
    42→- Move rootFolderKey to IPFS vault record -- server stores zero crypto material, true zero-knowledge relay
    43→- BYO-IPFS node support via Pinning Service API -- unique among encrypted storage apps, enables self-sovereignty over data persistence
    44→- End-to-end user journey performance baselines -- login-to-vault, upload-to-visible timing
    45→- IPFS/IPNS latency histograms with per-operation breakdown in Prometheus
    46→
    47→**Defer (v1.2+):**
    48→
    49→- CRDT-based share discovery via IPNS inbox -- research-only in v1.1, architecturally desirable but premature
    50→- Migrate device registry off DB -- requires solving approval handshake without server coordination
    51→- Eliminate `pinned_cids` table -- requires alternative quota tracking
    52→- Client-direct IPFS upload mode -- CORS issues, breaks quota tracking and conflict detection
    53→
    54→### Architecture Approach
    55→
    56→The architecture follows a "DB-first, IPFS-verify" pattern for IPNS resolution, a versioned blob format for metadata evolution, a provider factory pattern for BYO-IPFS extensibility, and additive histogram instrumentation for performance baselines. The key insight is that the DB already serves as the reliable source for IPNS CIDs -- making this explicit (instead of treating it as a fallback) simplifies the architecture and eliminates the external `delegated-ipfs.dev` dependency without adding new infrastructure.
    57→
    58→**Major components:**
    59→
    60→1. **KuboIpnsClient** -- New NestJS injectable wrapping Kubo's RPC API (`/api/v0/name/resolve`, `/api/v0/name/publish`, `/api/v0/key/import`). Handles native DHT resolution and Kubo-native publishing for the TEE republish path.
    61→2. **Vault Blob v2 Format** -- Extends the root IPNS blob with a version byte and ECIES-wrapped `encryptedRootFolderKey` header, enabling client-side key extraction without a DB round-trip. Version-aware reading allows graceful fallback to blob v1.
    62→3. **Provider Factory + DualPinProvider** -- Per-user `IpfsProviderFactory` that returns either the default `LocalProvider` or a `DualPinProvider` wrapping both `LocalProvider` (always) and `UserCustomProvider` (best-effort mirror to user's node).
    63→4. **MetricsService Extensions** -- 4 new Prometheus histograms: IPNS resolve duration, IPNS publish duration, IPFS operation duration, TEE republish batch duration. All use the existing `prom-client` infrastructure.
    64→
    65→### Critical Pitfalls
    66→
    67→1. **Sequence number divergence during routing provider migration** -- The new Kubo DHT endpoint may have no records initially. Run the new provider in read-parallel mode for 48+ hours before cutting writes. Dual-write to old and new providers during transition. Monitor for 409 Conflict error spikes.
    68→
    69→2. **rootFolderKey on IPNS creates login-critical IPNS dependency** -- Moving rootFolderKey exclusively to IPFS means login fails when IPNS is down. The mitigation is absolute: keep the DB copy as the primary source for login, use the IPFS copy for recovery tool independence. This is a "both, not either" situation. Never drop the DB column.
    70→
    71→3. **DHT record expiry during routing provider migration** -- IPNS records expire from the DHT after 48 hours regardless of the validity field. If both providers are misconfigured during a weekend deployment, ALL records expire simultaneously. Build an emergency republish endpoint that bypasses the 6-hour schedule.
    72→
    73→4. **BYO-IPFS bypasses server-side optimistic concurrency** -- If users publish IPNS records directly to their node (bypassing the API), the server cannot check sequence numbers. The fix: BYO-IPFS affects ONLY where data is pinned, never how metadata is published. All IPNS publishes must still go through the CipherBox API.
    74→
    75→5. **Quota tracking becomes unenforceable with BYO-IPFS** -- Server cannot meter uploads it does not relay. For BYO users, skip server-side quota enforcement and require client-reported CID sizes. Mark `pinned_cids` entries with a `provider` column to distinguish server-pinned from BYO-pinned.
    76→
    77→## Implications for Roadmap
    78→
    79→Based on research, suggested phase structure:
    80→
    81→### Phase 1: Performance Instrumentation
    82→
    83→**Rationale:** Zero risk to existing functionality. Purely additive. Establishes "before" measurements that Phase 2-4 can be compared against. Without baselines, we cannot prove that subsequent phases improve performance or detect regressions.
    84→**Delivers:** 4 new Prometheus histograms (IPNS resolve/publish duration, IPFS operation duration, TEE republish batch duration), client-side timing utility, baseline measurements document, k6 load test scripts.
    85→**Addresses:** Table stakes features -- IPNS publish latency monitoring, API endpoint baselines.
    86→**Avoids:** Pitfall P7 (instrumentation overhead) -- verify overhead < 5% on P95 latency with and without instrumentation.
    87→**Estimated scope:** Small. ~10 modified files, no new entities or migrations.
    88→
    89→### Phase 2: IPNS Resolution Improvement
    90→
    91→**Rationale:** Fixes the most critical reliability issue and is the gating dependency for Phase 3. The current `delegated-ipfs.dev` dependency causes 502 errors and stale records. Must be resolved before making IPNS a login-critical path.
    92→**Delivers:** `KuboIpnsClient` for native Kubo DHT resolution, DB-first resolution strategy, `delegated-ipfs.dev` demoted to disabled-by-default fallback, Kubo config with `Ipns.UsePubsub: true`, updated recovery tool resolution chain.
    93→**Addresses:** Table stakes -- reliable IPNS resolution, graceful degradation.
    94→**Avoids:** Pitfall P1 (sequence number divergence) -- run dual-provider for 48+ hours, compare sequence numbers across providers. Pitfall P5 (DHT record expiry) -- never take old provider offline until new one has processed a full republish cycle.
    95→**Estimated scope:** Medium. 1 new file, ~5 modified files, Kubo config change, recovery tool update.
    96→
    97→### Phase 3: Database Minimization (rootFolderKey to IPFS)
    98→
    99→**Rationale:** Requires reliable IPNS resolution from Phase 2. This is the highest-value zero-knowledge improvement: the server stores zero crypto material after migration. The vault blob v2 format is a breaking metadata change requiring careful migration across web, desktop, and recovery tool.
   100→**Delivers:** Vault blob v2 format with embedded `encryptedRootFolderKey`, version-aware blob reading, dual-write migration strategy, `encryptedRootIpnsPrivateKey` deprecation, updated recovery tool, metadata schema version bump per METADATA_EVOLUTION_PROTOCOL.
   101→**Addresses:** Differentiator -- server stores zero crypto material, true zero-knowledge relay.
   102→**Avoids:** Pitfall P2 (rootFolderKey on IPNS) -- keep DB copy as permanent fallback for login, use IPFS copy for recovery tool independence. Pitfall P3 (share discovery on IPFS) -- explicitly do NOT migrate shares, keep sharing graph in DB.
   103→**Estimated scope:** Large. New type definitions, serialization code, changes across 3 clients (web, desktop, recovery).
   104→
   105→### Phase 4: BYO-IPFS Node Support
   106→
   107→**Rationale:** Largest surface area but most independent feature. Benefits from stable IPNS resolution (Phase 2) and reduced DB dependency (Phase 3) but does not strictly require them. The `RemotePinningProvider` is additive -- it mirrors pins without replacing the local provider.
   108→**Delivers:** `UserCustomProvider` (Kubo RPC + Pinning Service API), `DualPinProvider` wrapper, `ProviderFactory` for per-user resolution, `user_ipfs_config` entity and migration, IPFS config CRUD API endpoints, connection test endpoint, Settings page UI for IPFS node configuration, BYO-specific Prometheus metrics.
   109→**Addresses:** Differentiator -- BYO-IPFS node support, unique among encrypted storage apps.
   110→**Avoids:** Pitfall P4 (BYO bypasses concurrency) -- all IPNS publishes still go through API, BYO affects only pinning. Pitfall P6 (quota tracking) -- skip server-side quota for BYO users, require client-reported CID sizes, add `provider` column to `pinned_cids`.
   111→**Estimated scope:** Large. New entity, migration, 4+ new provider files, UI component, multiple API endpoints.
   112→
   113→### Phase Ordering Rationale
   114→
   115→- **Phase 1 before everything:** Baselines must exist before changes, or you cannot measure impact. Zero dependencies, zero risk.
   116→- **Phase 2 before Phase 3 (hard dependency):** Moving rootFolderKey to IPFS makes IPNS resolution login-critical. IPNS must be reliable first. All four research files independently flag this as the most important ordering constraint.
   117→- **Phase 3 before Phase 4 (soft dependency):** Not strictly required, but Phase 3 reduces DB dependency which aligns with BYO-IPFS goals. Phase 4's design work can proceed in parallel with Phase 3's implementation.
   118→- **Phase 4 last:** Largest surface area, most new code, benefits from all prior infrastructure improvements.
   119→
   120→### Research Flags
   121→
   122→Phases likely needing deeper research during planning:
   123→
   124→- **Phase 3 (Database Minimization):** Migration protocol edge cases -- blob v2 format across 3 clients, dual-write/dual-read window management, recovery tool independence, desktop (Rust) blob parsing. This is the riskiest phase.
   125→- **Phase 4 (BYO-IPFS):** Per-user provider routing in NestJS DI, IPFS Pinning Service API real-world testing, auth token storage model (ECIES-wrapped vs server-encrypted), Settings UI design.
   126→
   127→Phases with standard patterns (skip research-phase):
   128→
   129→- **Phase 1 (Performance Instrumentation):** Established `prom-client` + `MetricsService` pattern. k6 is well-documented. Purely additive.
   130→- **Phase 2 (IPNS Resolution):** Kubo RPC API is documented, DB-first resolution is architecturally straightforward. The main risk (sequence number divergence during transition) is an operational concern, not a research gap.
   131→
   132→## Confidence Assessment
   133→
   134→| Area         | Confidence                                               | Notes                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
   135→| ------------ | -------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
   136→| Stack        | HIGH                                                     | Zero new npm dependencies. All capabilities use existing libraries. Kubo API contracts verified against official docs.                                                                                                       |
   137→| Features     | HIGH for IPNS/perf, MEDIUM for DB migration and BYO-IPFS | IPNS reliability and performance monitoring are well-documented domains. DB-to-IPFS migration has app-specific edge cases. BYO-IPFS Pinning Service API is well-specified but per-user provider routing patterns are sparse. |
   138→| Architecture | HIGH                                                     | Existing codebase analyzed in detail. DB-first resolution, provider factory, blob versioning are all established patterns. Build order validated against dependency graph.                                                   |
   139→| Pitfalls     | HIGH                                                     | 6 critical pitfalls identified with specific prevention strategies, warning signs, and recovery plans. Phase-to-pitfall mapping is complete.                                                                                 |
   140→
   141→**Overall confidence:** HIGH
   142→
   143→### Gaps to Address
   144→
   145→- **rootFolderKey migration dual-write window:** How long should the dual-write period last? What happens to users who never log in during the window? Need a forced migration strategy (background job that reads DB key, writes blob v2, publishes to IPNS) for dormant accounts.
   146→- **BYO-IPFS auth token storage model:** STACK.md recommends server-side encryption (server needs to decrypt to call user's node). ARCHITECTURE.md implements this. PITFALLS.md warns about server compromise exposing tokens. The tradeoff (server sees token but not plaintext content) needs explicit acceptance and documentation.
   147→- **Kubo version decision:** v0.34.0 works but v0.40.1 is recommended. The upgrade should happen before Phase 2 starts but is not blocking. Need to validate that Kubo v0.40.1 does not introduce breaking changes for the existing `LocalProvider` calls.
   148→- **CRDT inbox for shares:** Research-only this milestone. Need to document the CRDT approach as a design RFC during Phase 3 work so it feeds into v1.2 planning.
   149→- **Recovery tool independence:** The recovery tool currently resolves IPNS via `delegated-ipfs.dev` directly from the browser. Phases 2 and 3 both modify its behavior. Need to verify it works WITHOUT the CipherBox API running after all changes.
   150→- **IPNS routing approach reconciliation:** STACK.md recommends Kubo's `Gateway.ExposeRoutingAPI`, FEATURES.md recommends self-hosted Someguy, ARCHITECTURE.md recommends DB-first with Kubo RPC `/api/v0/name/resolve`. Adopt the ARCHITECTURE.md approach (DB-first with async Kubo DHT verification) as the definitive strategy -- it is the most robust because it makes the already-reliable DB the primary path and uses Kubo DHT only for background verification.
   151→
   152→## Sources
   153→
   154→### Primary (HIGH confidence)
   155→
   156→- [Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API Spec](https://specs.ipfs.tech/routing/http-routing-v1/) -- IPNS endpoint contract
   157→- [IPFS Pinning Service API Spec](https://ipfs.github.io/pinning-services-api-spec/) -- BYO-IPFS integration standard

   159→- [Kubo Configuration Reference](https://github.com/ipfs/kubo/blob/master/docs/config.md) -- `Ipns.UsePubsub`, `Routing.Type`, `Gateway.ExposeRoutingAPI`
   160→- [Kubo v0.34.0](https://github.com/ipfs/kubo/releases/tag/v0.34.0) and [v0.40.0](https://github.com/ipfs/kubo/releases/tag/v0.40.0) Release Notes
   161→- [Someguy GitHub](https://github.com/ipfs/someguy) -- Self-hosted delegated routing reference

     2→
     3→**Domain:** Adding document editors, team accounts, billing, and document signing to a zero-knowledge encrypted storage system (CipherBox)
     4→**Researched:** 2026-02-11

     7→---
     8→
     9→## Context: Why M3 Is Uniquely Dangerous
    10→
    11→CipherBox's existing architecture enforces a strict invariant: the server never sees plaintext. Every key is either held in client RAM or wrapped with ECIES using the user's public key. IPNS metadata is encrypted with per-folder AES-256-GCM keys. The entire key hierarchy flows from a single user's secp256k1 keypair derived by Web3Auth.
    12→
    13→Milestone 4 introduces four features that each, in different ways, pressure this invariant:
    14→
    15→- **Document editors** require real-time state that must be encrypted at rest but editable in real time
```

     1→# Milestone 4 Research Summary
     2→
     3→**Project:** CipherBox -- Encrypted Productivity Suite
     4→**Domain:** Zero-knowledge document editing, team accounts, billing, document signing
     5→**Researched:** 2026-02-11
     6→**Confidence:** MEDIUM
     7→
     8→## Executive Summary
     9→
    10→Milestone 4 transforms CipherBox from an encrypted file locker into an encrypted productivity platform. The four feature pillars -- billing, team accounts, document editors, and document signing -- vary dramatically in complexity. Billing (Stripe + optional crypto payments) is straightforward with well-documented patterns. Team accounts introduce a real key hierarchy challenge but follow established models from Keybase and Tresorit. Document editors are the highest-risk, highest-effort feature: integrating TipTap + Yjs for rich text editing with encrypted CRDT transport is novel territory that CryptPad and Proton Docs each spent years building. Document signing is the simplest win, leveraging existing Web3Auth secp256k1 keys for ECDSA attestation.
    11→
    12→The recommended approach is to build in strict dependency order: Billing first (enables tier gating), then Teams (enables shared vaults), then Document Editors (the flagship feature), then Signing (additive to existing documents). This order minimizes rework -- each phase builds on the previous one. Spreadsheets should ship as single-user-editable only (no real-time collaboration), and slides should be deferred entirely. Real-time collaboration for documents should also be deferred to M4+; M3 should implement single-user editing with advisory locking for team documents.
    13→
    14→The critical risks center on two threats to the zero-knowledge guarantee: editor libraries leaking plaintext through collaboration protocols, and team key distribution introducing server-visible key material. Both are solvable with known patterns (encrypted CRDT transport, ECIES-wrapped per-team keys) but require deliberate architecture -- the path of least resistance in every editor and team library assumes a trusted server. Autosave conflicting with IPNS publish latency is a secondary but real risk that needs a save queue with mutual exclusion from day one.
    15→
```

Five phases follow the natural dependency chain: billing infrastructure (gates tier-based access), team accounts (enables shared vaults with ECIES-wrapped Per-Team Keys), document editors (TipTap rich text + Univer spreadsheets with decrypt-edit-encrypt pipeline), document signing (ECDSA attestation using existing Web3Auth keys), and AWS Nitro TEE (fallback republisher moved from M2).     32→
    33→1. User can select a plan (Free/Pro/Team), complete Stripe Checkout, and see their subscription activated within seconds of payment
    34→2. User can open Stripe Customer Portal from settings to upgrade, downgrade, or cancel their subscription without contacting support
    35→3. Pro user uploading files beyond the free 500 MiB limit succeeds up to 50 GiB; free user is blocked at 500 MiB with an upgrade prompt
    36→4. User can pay with cryptocurrency (BTC, ETH, USDC) via NOWPayments; subscription activates after blockchain confirmation
    37→5. When a user's payment fails, they retain access for a grace period and receive notification; access downgrades only after grace period expires
    38→
    39→**Plans**: TBD
    40→
    41→### Phase 19: Team Accounts
    42→
    43→**Goal**: Users can create teams with shared encrypted vaults where team members access shared content through a zero-knowledge key hierarchy
    44→**Depends on**: Phase 18 (billing tier enforcement for team member limits)
    45→**Requirements**: TEAM-01, TEAM-02, TEAM-03, TEAM-04, TEAM-05, TEAM-06, TEAM-07, TEAM-08, TEAM-09
    46→**Research flag**: NEEDS `/gsd:research-phase` -- PTK rotation on member removal is the most complex protocol in M4. ECIES re-wrapping correctness with key epochs must be validated. IPNS record size under team metadata growth (N members x M wrapped keys) needs measurement.
    47→**Success Criteria** (what must be TRUE):
    48→
    49→1. User can create a team, and the team vault is initialized with a team-scoped IPNS keypair and root folder key (all encrypted with the Per-Team Key, never plaintext on server)
    50→2. Owner can invite a member by email or public key; the PTK is ECIES-wrapped to the invitee's publicKey; invitee can accept and immediately browse team files
    51→3. User can switch between personal vault and team vaults in the sidebar; folder navigation within a team vault works identically to the personal vault
    52→4. Owner can remove a member, which triggers PTK rotation -- the removed member can no longer decrypt newly encrypted team content
    53→5. Team of 25 members on a Team tier operates without hitting member limits; free/pro users attempting to create teams are prompted to upgrade
    54→
    55→**Plans**: TBD
    56→
    57→### Phase 20: Document Editors
    58→
    59→**Goal**: Users can create and edit rich text documents and spreadsheets directly in the browser with all content encrypted at rest on IPFS
    60→**Depends on**: Phase 19 (team vaults for shared document editing context and advisory locking)
    61→**Requirements**: EDIT-01, EDIT-02, EDIT-03, EDIT-04, EDIT-05, EDIT-06, EDIT-07, EDIT-08, EDIT-09
    62→**Research flag**: NEEDS `/gsd:research-phase` -- TipTap 3.x + Univer integration specifics, bundle size impact, autosave pipeline performance with IPFS/IPNS latency. A proof-of-concept of the decrypt-edit-encrypt pipeline should be built early in planning.
    63→**Success Criteria** (what must be TRUE):
    64→
    65→1. User can create a new document, type and format text (bold, italic, headings, lists, links, tables), and the content is encrypted and persisted to IPFS on save
    66→2. User can reopen a previously saved document and see their content exactly as they left it (round-trip through encrypt/decrypt preserves formatting)
    67→3. User can create and edit a spreadsheet with formulas, formatting, and multiple sheets; spreadsheet state is encrypted and persisted identically to documents
    68→4. Autosave fires after 60 seconds of inactivity; a dirty indicator shows unsaved changes; closing the tab with unsaved changes triggers a warning
    69→5. When a team member opens a team document for editing, other team members see an advisory lock indicator and can choose read-only view or force-edit
    70→
    71→**Plans**: TBD
    72→
    73→### Phase 21: Document Signing
    74→
    75→**Goal**: Users can cryptographically sign documents with their Web3Auth keys and any authorized party can verify the signature
    76→**Depends on**: Phase 20 (documents exist to be signed; editor infrastructure provides the document viewing context)
    77→**Requirements**: SIGN-01, SIGN-02, SIGN-03, SIGN-04, SIGN-05, SIGN-06
    78→**Research flag**: Standard patterns -- uses existing ECDSA primitives (secp256k1 via Web3Auth + Web Crypto API). Straightforward implementation. Skip `/gsd:research-phase`.
    79→**Success Criteria** (what must be TRUE):
    80→
    81→1. User can sign any document or file, producing an ECDSA signature over the SHA-256 content hash; signature metadata is stored in encrypted folder metadata
    82→2. Any user with folder access can verify a signature is valid for the given document content and signer's publicKey
    83→3. User can draw or type a visual signature that is displayed alongside the cryptographic signature status in the file browser
    84→4. User can request signatures from other CipherBox users; the document shows signature status (pending/partial/complete) and lists who has and has not signed
    85→5. User can export a signed document as a PDF with the digital signature embedded (client-side via LibPDF)
    86→
    87→**Plans**: TBD
    88→
    89→### Phase 22: AWS Nitro TEE
    90→
    91→**Goal**: IPNS republishing has a fallback TEE provider for high availability (moved from M2 — Phala Cloud mock still in use on staging)
    92→**Depends on**: Phase 12 (can run in parallel with Phases 18-21)
    93→**Requirements**: TEE-06

    95→**Success Criteria** (what must be TRUE):
    96→
    97→1. AWS Nitro enclave can receive ECIES-encrypted IPNS keys, decrypt in hardware, sign IPNS records, and zero memory
    98→2. Backend routes republish jobs to Nitro when Phala Cloud is unavailable, with automatic failover and failback
    99→

     1→# Milestone 4 Domain Pitfalls
     2→
     3→**Domain:** Adding document editors, team accounts, billing, and document signing to a zero-knowledge encrypted storage system (CipherBox)
     4→**Researched:** 2026-02-11
     5→**Overall Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH
     6→
     7→---
     8→
     9→## Context: Why M3 Is Uniquely Dangerous
    10→
    11→CipherBox's existing architecture enforces a strict invariant: the server never sees plaintext. Every key is either held in client RAM or wrapped with ECIES using the user's public key. IPNS metadata is encrypted with per-folder AES-256-GCM keys. The entire key hierarchy flows from a single user's secp256k1 keypair derived by Web3Auth.
    12→
    13→Milestone 4 introduces four features that each, in different ways, pressure this invariant:
    14→
    15→- **Document editors** require real-time state that must be encrypted at rest but editable in real time
    16→- **Team accounts** require multiple users to decrypt the same data without a shared private key
    17→- **Billing** requires the server to enforce access tiers on data it cannot inspect
    18→- **Document signing** requires cryptographic proof of authorship within an encrypted context
    19→
    20→The overarching risk: every M3 feature creates a temptation to "just let the server see a little bit." Each small leak compounds into a broken zero-knowledge guarantee.
    21→
    22→---
    23→
    24→## Critical Pitfalls
    25→
    26→Mistakes in this category cause architectural rewrites, security violations, or loss of the zero-knowledge guarantee.
    27→
    28→### C1: Editor Library Leaking Plaintext to the Server
    29→
    30→**What goes wrong:** A rich text editor (TipTap, ProseMirror, Slate, CKEditor) is integrated with a collaboration backend. The collaboration protocol (WebSocket sync, Yjs provider, or custom OT) transmits document operations in cleartext to a relay server. The server now has full access to document contents, destroying the ZK guarantee. This can happen subtly -- for example, a Yjs WebSocket provider sends unencrypted updates by default, or a collaboration server-side component logs operations for debugging.
    31→
    32→**Why it happens:** All mainstream collaboration libraries assume a trusted server model. Yjs's y-websocket provider, Liveblocks, TipTap Cloud, and Hocuspocus all relay plaintext operations through a central server. The path of least resistance is to use these out of the box. CryptPad solved this by building ChainPad from scratch, and Proton Docs reportedly took over a year to build their encrypted collaboration layer.
    33→
    34→#### Consequences
    35→
    36→- Complete loss of zero-knowledge guarantee
    37→- Every keystroke, cursor movement, and formatting change visible to server
    38→- Retroactive breach: if server logs were captured before the leak was noticed, historical document content is exposed
    39→
    40→#### Warning Signs
    41→
    42→- Any WebSocket message from client to server containing document content that is not encrypted
    43→- Collaboration provider documentation that does not mention client-side encryption
    44→- Server-side code that deserializes or inspects CRDT/OT operations
    45→- Using a hosted collaboration service (Liveblocks, TipTap Cloud) that requires seeing document state
    46→
    47→#### Prevention
    48→
    49→1. All CRDT/OT operations must be encrypted client-side before transmission. Yjs supports custom providers -- build one that encrypts updates with the document's folderKey (or a dedicated documentKey) before sending over WebSocket
    50→2. The relay server must be a dumb pipe: receives opaque encrypted blobs, broadcasts to other participants, never parses content
    51→3. Audit every network request from the editor. Use browser DevTools to verify no plaintext leaks in WebSocket frames
    52→4. Never use hosted collaboration backends (TipTap Cloud, Liveblocks Cloud, Hocuspocus) that require server-side document access
    53→
    54→**Confidence:** HIGH -- this is a well-documented challenge. CryptPad, Proton Docs, and Skiff (now defunct) all solved it by encrypting at the transport layer.
    55→
    56→**Feature area:** Document Editors
    57→
    58→---
    59→
    60→### C2: Team Key Distribution Without a Trusted Server
    61→
    62→**What goes wrong:** When implementing team accounts, the natural approach is to store a "team key" on the server and distribute it to team members. But in a ZK system, the server cannot hold plaintext keys. Teams require that User A can grant User B access to encrypted data, but the server facilitating this exchange must never see the key material. Getting this wrong means either (a) the server holds team keys in plaintext, or (b) key distribution is so complex it becomes a source of bugs that leak keys.
    63→
    64→**Why it happens:** Traditional team key management uses a key escrow model where a server encrypts/decrypts on behalf of users. In ZK, you must use asymmetric wrapping: the team key is encrypted separately for each member using their public key (ECIES). This means:
    65→
    66→- Adding a member requires re-wrapping the team key for the new member's public key
    67→- Removing a member requires rotating the team key and re-wrapping for all remaining members
    68→- The key rotation on member removal is the hard part -- all existing data encrypted with the old key must remain accessible (old key retained for decryption) while new data uses the new key
    69→
    70→CipherBox currently wraps all keys with a single user's publicKey. Extending this to N users per vault is a fundamental architectural change.
    71→
    72→#### Consequences
    73→
    74→- If team key is stored in plaintext on server: complete ZK violation
    75→- If key rotation on member removal is skipped: removed members retain decryption access forever (forward secrecy violation)
    76→- If key distribution has race conditions: members may end up with stale keys and lose access to newly encrypted data
    77→
    78→#### Warning Signs
    79→
    80→- Any database column storing a symmetric key that is not ECIES-wrapped
    81→- Member removal flow that does not include a key rotation step
    82→- Team vault creation that stores a single copy of the team key (should be N copies, one per member)
    83→
    84→#### Prevention
    85→
    86→1. Model team key distribution after Keybase's team key architecture: each team has a Per-Team Key (PTK) that is ECIES-wrapped for each member's publicKey. On member removal, rotate the PTK and re-wrap for remaining members
    87→2. Maintain a key epoch chain for the team: old epochs decrypt old data, current epoch encrypts new data. Never delete old epoch keys -- mark them as read-only
    88→3. Store the wrapped team keys in the team metadata IPNS record, so the server only sees ECIES-wrapped blobs
    89→4. Test the member removal + re-encryption flow exhaustively. This is where Keybase, Tresorit, and others have found the most bugs
    90→
    91→**Confidence:** HIGH -- Keybase published detailed documentation on their team crypto architecture, and Tresorit uses a similar model for business accounts.
    92→
    93→**Feature area:** Team Accounts
    94→
    95→---
    96→
    97→### C3: Autosave Conflicting with Encryption Round-Trips
    98→
    99→**What goes wrong:** A document editor autosaves every few seconds. In CipherBox, each save requires: (1) serialize document state, (2) encrypt with AES-256-GCM, (3) upload encrypted blob to IPFS via backend, (4) update IPNS record with new CID. This pipeline takes 2-5 seconds per save (encryption + IPFS add + IPNS publish). If the user makes another edit before the previous save completes, you get race conditions: stale IPNS records, lost edits, or corrupted metadata.
   100→
   101→**Why it happens:** Google Docs autosaves via a simple HTTP PATCH to a trusted server. CipherBox must encrypt, upload to IPFS (content-addressed, immutable), and publish a new IPNS record (mutable pointer update). The IPNS publish step alone can take 1-12 seconds depending on network conditions. Standard autosave intervals (Google Docs uses 30 seconds for encrypted files; typical editors use 2-5 seconds) conflict with this latency.
   102→
   103→#### Consequences
   104→
   105→- Lost edits when concurrent saves overwrite each other
   106→- IPNS sequence number conflicts (two publishes with same sequence number, network picks one arbitrarily)
   107→- Corrupted folder metadata when parent IPNS record is updated while a child save is in-flight
   108→- User-visible lag between typing and "saved" indicator, causing anxiety and retry behavior
   109→
   110→#### Warning Signs
   111→
   112→- Autosave timer fires while a previous save is still in-flight
   113→- IPNS publish errors with "sequence number" conflicts
   114→- Inconsistent document state after rapid editing sessions
   115→- Users reporting "lost changes" after editing sessions
   116→
   117→#### Prevention
   118→
   119→1. Implement a save queue with mutual exclusion: only one save can be in-flight at a time. Subsequent saves are coalesced (latest state wins) and executed after the current save completes
   120→2. Separate the collaboration layer (CRDT sync between clients) from the persistence layer (IPNS save). CRDTs sync in real time via encrypted WebSocket; IPNS persistence happens on a debounced schedule (e.g., 10-30 seconds of inactivity)
   121→3. Use optimistic local state: the editor shows the user their local CRDT state immediately, while IPNS persistence happens asynchronously in the background
   122→4. Implement IPNS sequence number tracking: always fetch current sequence number before publishing, increment by 1, and retry on conflict
   123→5. Consider storing CRDT update history separately from the "final document" snapshot. The CRDT log can be synced more frequently; the IPNS snapshot is the "checkpoint"
   124→
   125→**Confidence:** HIGH -- this is a direct consequence of CipherBox's existing IPNS architecture (30-second polling, sequence number conflicts) applied to a much more frequent write pattern.
   126→
   127→**Feature area:** Document Editors
   128→
   129→---
   130→
   131→### C4: Admin Access That Breaks Zero-Knowledge
   132→
   133→**What goes wrong:** Team accounts need an "admin" role that can manage the team, reset member access, and potentially recover data when a member loses their key. The naive implementation gives the admin a master key that decrypts everything, stored on the server for "recovery" purposes. This creates a backdoor that destroys ZK.
   134→
   135→**Why it happens:** Every enterprise customer asks for admin recovery. "What if an employee leaves and we need their files?" In a traditional system, the server can reset passwords and grant access. In ZK, the server genuinely cannot do this. The temptation is to add a server-side recovery key, an admin escrow key, or a "break glass" mechanism that undermines the entire security model.
   136→
   137→#### Consequences
   138→
   139→- Server-stored admin key = server can decrypt team data = not zero-knowledge
   140→- Regulatory exposure: claiming ZK while having a backdoor is potentially fraudulent
   141→- One compromised admin key exposes all team data
   142→
   143→#### Warning Signs
   144→
   145→- Any "admin recovery key" stored in the database without being ECIES-wrapped
   146→- Admin role that can access data without the admin's own private key being present in client RAM
   147→- Recovery flow that does not require the admin to be authenticated and present
   148→
   149→#### Prevention
   150→
   151→1. Use Tresorit's model: a designated "Recovery Administrator" holds a recovery key that is itself ECIES-wrapped with the admin's publicKey. Recovery requires the admin to be authenticated (private key in RAM) and is an explicit, auditable action
   152→2. Team creation generates a "team recovery key" that is ECIES-wrapped for each admin's publicKey. At least one admin must be present to recover any team data
   153→3. Accept the security/convenience tradeoff explicitly: if all admins lose their keys, team data is unrecoverable. This is a feature, not a bug, of zero-knowledge. Document this clearly for users
   154→4. Implement "key escrow with split custody" if regulatory requirements demand it: the recovery key is split (Shamir's Secret Sharing) across N admins, requiring K-of-N to reconstruct. No single party (including the server) can reconstruct alone
   155→
   156→**Confidence:** HIGH -- Tresorit, Keybase, and Bitwarden all document this pattern. Bitwarden's emergency access feature is a well-studied reference implementation.
   157→
   158→**Feature area:** Team Accounts
   159→
   160→---
   161→
   162→### C5: CRDT Tombstone Bloat in Encrypted Documents
   163→
   164→**What goes wrong:** CRDTs (specifically Yjs, the most likely choice) never truly delete data. When a user deletes text, the CRDT marks it as a "tombstone" -- the metadata about the deletion persists indefinitely to maintain consistency across clients. Over time, a document that has been heavily edited accumulates enormous tombstone overhead. In a ZK system, this bloated state must be encrypted, uploaded to IPFS, and published to IPNS with every save.
   165→
   166→**Why it happens:** CRDTs guarantee eventual consistency by keeping a complete history of all operations, including deletions. Yjs mitigates this by merging adjacent tombstones, but the overhead still grows linearly with edit history. A document edited by 5 people over months can have a CRDT state 10-50x larger than the visible document content. CryptPad addresses this with "checkpoints" every 50 patches, but this adds complexity.
   167→
   168→#### Consequences
   169→
   170→- Document load times increase as CRDT state grows (must decrypt and parse entire state)
   171→- IPFS storage costs increase (each save creates a new CID with the full bloated state)
   172→- Memory pressure in the browser: large CRDT states consume significant RAM, compounding with encryption overhead
   173→- IPNS metadata records may exceed practical size limits
   174→
   175→#### Warning Signs
   176→
   177→- Document CRDT state size growing faster than visible document size
   178→- Increasing load times for documents that have been edited many times
   179→- IPFS pin count and storage usage growing faster than expected
   180→- Browser memory warnings during editing sessions
   181→
   182→#### Prevention
   183→
   184→1. Implement periodic CRDT compaction/checkpointing: every N operations (CryptPad uses 50), create a "checkpoint" that is a fresh CRDT state initialized from the current document content, discarding tombstone history. Encrypt and store this checkpoint as the new base state
   185→2. Use Yjs subdocuments to split large documents into sections. Each section is a separate Yjs document that can be loaded lazily and compacted independently
   186→3. Set maximum document size limits and warn users before they hit them
   187→4. Separate the CRDT operation log (for real-time sync) from the persisted document state (for IPNS storage). Persist compacted snapshots, not full operation histories
   188→5. Monitor CRDT state size relative to document content size. Alert when the ratio exceeds a threshold (e.g., 5x)
   189→
   190→**Confidence:** MEDIUM -- Yjs tombstone behavior is well-documented, but the interaction with IPFS/IPNS persistence is specific to CipherBox and has not been tested at scale.
   191→
   192→**Feature area:** Document Editors
   193→
   194→---
   195→
   196→## Moderate Pitfalls
   197→
   198→Mistakes in this category cause delays, rework, or degraded user experience but do not break the security model.
   199→
   200→### M1: Billing State Desynchronization
   201→
   202→**What goes wrong:** The billing system (Stripe) and CipherBox's internal state disagree about a user's subscription status. A user's payment fails, Stripe marks them as "past_due," but CipherBox still shows them as active (or vice versa). Because CipherBox is ZK, the server cannot inspect file contents to enforce quotas -- it can only gate API access. If the gating logic is wrong, users either lose access prematurely or get unlimited free access.
   203→
   204→**Why it happens:** Stripe communicates subscription changes via webhooks. Webhooks can be delayed, duplicated, or arrive out of order. If CipherBox's webhook handler is not idempotent, duplicate events cause double-processing. If the handler does not verify webhook signatures, attackers can forge subscription events. If the handler processes events synchronously, a slow database write can cause Stripe to retry, creating duplicates.
   205→
   206→#### Consequences
   207→
   208→- Users lose access to their vault when their subscription is actually active (support tickets, churn)
   209→- Users retain access after subscription ends (revenue loss)
   210→- Race conditions during plan changes (upgrade/downgrade) leave users in inconsistent states
   211→- Free tier abuse: users create multiple accounts to bypass storage limits
   212→
   213→#### Warning Signs
   214→
   215→- Stripe webhook endpoint returning non-200 status codes
   216→- Subscription status in database not matching Stripe dashboard
   217→- Users reporting access issues after payment
   218→- Storage quota checks that query Stripe directly instead of using cached state
   219→
   220→#### Prevention
   221→
   222→1. Implement idempotent webhook processing: store a unique event ID for every processed Stripe webhook, reject duplicates
   223→2. Treat Stripe as the source of truth for billing state. On any ambiguity, query Stripe API directly (with caching)
   224→3. Implement a periodic reconciliation job that compares CipherBox subscription states with Stripe states and fixes discrepancies
   225→4. Use Stripe's `checkout.session.completed` and `customer.subscription.updated` events as the primary state change signals, not payment events
   226→5. Gate access based on subscription status in CipherBox's database (synced from Stripe), not by querying Stripe on every request
   227→
   228→**Confidence:** HIGH -- this is a well-known SaaS billing pitfall documented extensively in Stripe's own documentation.
   229→
   230→**Feature area:** Billing
   231→
   232→---
   233→
   234→### M2: Spreadsheet and Slides Complexity Explosion
   235→
   236→**What goes wrong:** After successfully building a document editor, the team assumes spreadsheets and slides will follow the same pattern. They do not. Spreadsheets require a formula engine, cell dependency graph, real-time recalculation, and collaborative cursor tracking across a 2D grid. Slides require a 2D canvas editor with layout constraints, shape manipulation, and presentation mode. Each is essentially a separate application, not a variant of a rich text editor.
   237→
   238→**Why it happens:** The PRD lists "docs/sheets/slides" as a single milestone item. This framing implies similar scope. In reality, even Proton (with 400+ engineers) has Docs available now but Sheets only recently shipped and Slides is not expected until late 2026. Google spent years building each app separately with dedicated teams.
   239→
   240→#### Consequences
   241→
   242→- Milestone 4 scope explodes to 3-5x the estimated effort
   243→- Half-built spreadsheet or slides implementation that lacks basic features (e.g., no formula support, no slide transitions)
   244→- Team burnout from underestimated scope
   245→
   246→#### Warning Signs
   247→
   248→- Milestone estimate for "docs + sheets + slides" is similar to estimate for docs alone
   249→- Evaluating a single editor library to cover all three use cases
   250→- No separate technical design documents for sheets and slides
   251→
   252→#### Prevention
   253→
   254→1. Scope M3 to document editing ONLY. Defer sheets and slides to separate milestones
   255→2. If sheets and slides are required, acknowledge that each needs its own editor library, data model, CRDT strategy, and encryption integration
   256→3. For spreadsheets specifically: evaluate whether a pure client-side formula engine exists that can work with encrypted data. Most formula engines (Hyperformula, FortuneSheet) assume trusted access to all cell values
   257→4. Consider leveraging existing open-source implementations (ONLYOFFICE, CryptPad) rather than building from scratch
   258→
   259→**Confidence:** HIGH -- Proton's public roadmap confirms this complexity. CryptPad took years to build sheets and slides.
   260→
   261→**Feature area:** Document Editors (Scope)
   262→
   263→---
   264→
   265→### M3: Encryption Performance Cliff on Large Documents
   266→
   267→**What goes wrong:** The document editor works well for small documents (a few KB). For large documents (100+ pages, embedded images, complex formatting), the encrypt-then-upload cycle becomes prohibitively slow. AES-256-GCM encryption of a 10MB document takes 50-200ms, but combined with JSON serialization, CRDT state packaging, IPFS upload, and IPNS publish, the total round-trip can take 5-15 seconds. During this time, the UI freezes or shows stale state.
   268→
   269→**Why it happens:** CipherBox's current architecture encrypts entire files atomically. For static files (PDFs, images), this is fine. For live documents that change continuously, re-encrypting the entire document state on every save is wasteful. Additionally, Web Crypto API runs on the main thread by default, blocking UI rendering during encryption.
   270→
   271→#### Consequences
   272→
   273→- UI jank and freezing during saves
   274→- Users perceive the editor as slow/broken
   275→- Autosave intervals must be increased, reducing data safety
   276→- Large documents become practically unusable
   277→
   278→#### Warning Signs
   279→
   280→- Encryption time exceeding 100ms for document saves
   281→- Main thread blocked during crypto operations
   282→- Users complaining about lag in the editor
   283→- Increasing save failure rate as document size grows
   284→
   285→#### Prevention
   286→
   287→1. Use Web Workers for all crypto operations. Offload AES-256-GCM encryption to a dedicated worker so the main thread remains responsive
   288→2. Implement incremental encryption: encrypt only the changed portions of the document (diff-based encryption). This requires splitting the document into encrypted chunks and only re-encrypting modified chunks
   289→3. Use Yjs's built-in update encoding: Yjs can produce compact "update" messages (just the diff) rather than full state snapshots. Encrypt and transmit only updates for real-time sync; do full-state snapshots less frequently
   290→4. Set document size limits (e.g., 10MB of content) and enforce them in the editor
   291→5. Stream large document content through the CTR mode encryption (from v1.1) rather than buffering the entire document in memory
   292→
   293→**Confidence:** MEDIUM -- the individual components (Web Crypto performance, IPFS upload latency, IPNS publish latency) are well-understood, but their combined impact on editor UX is specific to CipherBox's architecture.
   294→
   295→**Feature area:** Document Editors
   296→
   297→---
   298→
   299→### M4: Free Tier Abuse in a Zero-Knowledge System
   300→
   301→**What goes wrong:** Because the server cannot inspect file contents, it cannot distinguish between legitimate files and abuse vectors (e.g., using the free tier as a CDN for pirated content, storing encrypted malware, or creating multiple accounts to aggregate free storage). Traditional abuse detection relies on content scanning, which is impossible in ZK.
   302→
   303→**Why it happens:** ZK guarantees protect the user from the server -- but they also protect abusers from detection. The server can track metadata (file sizes, upload frequency, IP addresses) but cannot scan content. This creates a unique abuse surface that traditional billing abuse prevention does not address.
   304→
   305→#### Consequences
   306→
   307→- Storage costs balloon from abuse (IPFS pinning costs per GB)
   308→- Legal liability: server cannot comply with DMCA takedown requests for encrypted content
   309→- Reputation risk: platform used for illegal content storage
   310→- Legitimate users subsidize abusers via shared infrastructure costs
   311→
   312→#### Warning Signs
   313→
   314→- Free tier accounts consuming maximum storage limits
   315→- High upload frequency from single IP ranges
   316→- Accounts created in bulk (same email patterns, same device fingerprints)
   317→- Storage costs growing faster than user growth
   318→
   319→#### Prevention
   320→
   321→1. Rate-limit uploads by IP, device fingerprint, and account age (not by content inspection)
   322→2. Require email verification and consider phone verification for free accounts
   323→3. Implement progressive trust: new accounts get lower limits that increase with account age and payment history
   324→4. Track metadata-level signals: file count, total size, upload frequency, API call patterns. These do not violate ZK (server already tracks storage quotas)
   325→5. Make the free tier genuinely limited (CipherBox already has 500 MiB). Ensure this limit is server-enforced at the upload API level
   326→6. Accept that content-based abuse detection is impossible. Focus prevention on account-level controls
   327→
   328→**Confidence:** HIGH -- this is a known challenge for all ZK storage providers (Proton, Tresorit, MEGA).
   329→
   330→**Feature area:** Billing
   331→
   332→---
   333→
   334→### M5: E-Signature Legal Validity in a ZK Context
   335→
   336→**What goes wrong:** The team implements document signing using the user's existing ECDSA keypair (secp256k1 from Web3Auth). The signature is cryptographically valid but legally questionable because: (a) secp256k1 is not a standard recognized by eIDAS or most e-signature regulations, (b) there is no timestamp authority (TSA) to prove when the signature was made, (c) there is no certificate authority (CA) binding the public key to a verified identity, and (d) the signed document is encrypted, making third-party verification impossible without the decryption key.
   337→
   338→**Why it happens:** The team conflates "cryptographic signature" with "legally binding e-signature." Under ESIGN Act and UETA, e-signatures are broadly valid, but courts require proof of identity, intent, and timestamp. A secp256k1 signature proves that the holder of a private key signed something, but does not prove who that person is (no CA) or when they signed (no TSA).
   339→
   340→#### Consequences
   341→
   342→- Signatures rejected by counterparties or courts
   343→- Users believe their signatures are legally binding when they may not be
   344→- Regulatory liability if the product claims legal validity without meeting requirements
   345→- Integration complexity if a proper PKI must be retrofitted
   346→
   347→#### Warning Signs
   348→
   349→- Signing flow that uses only the Web3Auth keypair without additional identity verification
   350→- No integration with a timestamp authority (RFC 3161)
   351→- No way for a third party to verify a signature without having the document decryption key
   352→- Marketing materials claiming "legally binding" without legal review
   353→
   354→#### Prevention
   355→
   356→1. Distinguish between "cryptographic attestation" (user confirms they authored/approved a document) and "legal e-signature" (meets ESIGN/UETA/eIDAS requirements). For a tech demo, the former may suffice
   357→2. If legal validity is required, integrate with an established e-signature provider (DocuSign API, Adobe Sign) that handles identity verification, TSA, and audit trails
   358→3. For internal use (team members signing off on documents), the existing ECDSA signature plus an audit log of who signed what, when may be sufficient
   359→4. Create a "signature proof" document that includes: the document hash, the signature, the signer's public key, a timestamp from a trusted source, and instructions for verification. This proof can be shared without sharing the document itself
   360→5. Never use the IPNS signing key (Ed25519) for document signing. These keys are shared with the TEE for republishing and have a different trust model
   361→
   362→**Confidence:** HIGH -- e-signature legal requirements are well-documented. The ESIGN Act and eIDAS regulation are clear about requirements.
   363→
   364→**Feature area:** Document Signing
   365→
   366→---
   367→
   368→### M6: Webhook Security and Payment Fraud
   369→
   370→**What goes wrong:** The Stripe webhook endpoint accepts forged events because it does not verify Stripe's webhook signature. An attacker sends a fake `customer.subscription.updated` event, granting themselves a premium subscription. Alternatively, the webhook endpoint is not rate-limited and an attacker floods it with events, causing a denial of service.
   371→
   372→**Why it happens:** Webhook signature verification requires extracting the `Stripe-Signature` header and validating it against the webhook signing secret. Skipping this step during development is common, and it sometimes makes it to production.
   373→
   374→#### Consequences
   375→
   376→- Free premium access for attackers
   377→- Revenue loss from forged subscription events
   378→- Potential data corruption if forged events trigger account state changes
   379→
   380→#### Warning Signs
   381→
   382→- Webhook endpoint that does not call `stripe.webhooks.constructEvent()` with the signing secret
   383→- No rate limiting on the webhook endpoint
   384→- Webhook endpoint accessible without HTTPS
   385→
   386→#### Prevention
   387→
   388→1. Always verify Stripe webhook signatures using the official SDK
   389→2. Rate-limit the webhook endpoint
   390→3. Use Stripe's test mode to verify the full webhook flow before going live
   391→4. Log all webhook events for audit purposes
   392→5. Implement an event deduplication check (store event IDs, reject duplicates)
   393→
   394→**Confidence:** HIGH -- Stripe's documentation explicitly warns about this.
   395→
   396→**Feature area:** Billing
   397→
   398→---
   399→
   400→## Minor Pitfalls
   401→
   402→Mistakes in this category cause annoyance or minor UX issues but are straightforward to fix.
   403→
   404→### N1: Editor State Loss on Browser Tab Close
   405→
   406→**What goes wrong:** The user is editing a document. They close the browser tab or navigate away. The editor had unsaved changes that were in the CRDT local state but not yet persisted to IPNS. Those changes are lost.
   407→
   408→#### Prevention
   409→
   410→1. Implement `beforeunload` handler that warns users about unsaved changes
   411→2. Use IndexedDB (via Yjs's y-indexeddb provider) to persist CRDT state locally as a buffer. On tab reopen, recover from IndexedDB
   412→3. Debounce IPNS saves but save CRDT state to IndexedDB on every change (IndexedDB writes are fast and local)
   413→
   414→**Feature area:** Document Editors
   415→
   416→---
   417→
   418→### N2: Key Wrapping Cost Scaling with Team Size
   419→
   420→**What goes wrong:** Every file key and folder key in a team vault is ECIES-wrapped for each team member's public key. A team of 50 members means 50 ECIES wrapping operations per file upload. ECIES (secp256k1) is computationally expensive compared to AES.
   421→
   422→#### Prevention
   423→
   424→1. Use a two-level key hierarchy: wrap individual file/folder keys with the team's symmetric Per-Team Key (PTK), and wrap only the PTK for each member's public key. This reduces per-file ECIES operations from N to 1
   425→2. This is the standard approach used by Keybase, Tresorit, and Proton for team vaults
   426→
   427→**Feature area:** Team Accounts
   428→
   429→---
   430→
   431→### N3: Crypto Payment Integration Complexity
   432→
   433→**What goes wrong:** Adding cryptocurrency payment options (for a Web3-aligned product) introduces exchange rate volatility, transaction confirmation delays, and a completely separate payment infrastructure alongside Stripe.
   434→
   435→#### Prevention
   436→
   437→1. Defer crypto payments to a later milestone. Start with Stripe only
   438→2. If crypto payments are required, use a payment processor that handles crypto-to-fiat conversion (e.g., Coinbase Commerce, BTCPay Server) rather than managing wallets directly
   439→3. Never store crypto payment details in the same database as vault metadata
   440→
   441→**Feature area:** Billing
   442→
   443→---
   444→
   445→### N4: Certificate Management for Document Signing
   446→
   447→**What goes wrong:** If using PKI-based document signing (X.509 certificates), certificate expiration and renewal become operational burdens. Expired certificates invalidate all signatures made with them unless timestamps were applied.
   448→
   449→#### Prevention
   450→
   451→1. Always use a timestamp authority (TSA) when creating signatures. This ensures signatures remain valid even after the signing certificate expires
   452→2. Use a managed certificate service rather than self-hosting a CA
   453→3. If using the existing Web3Auth keypair for signing, skip certificates entirely and use a simpler attestation model
   454→
   455→**Feature area:** Document Signing
   456→
   457→---
   458→
   459→## Phase-Specific Warnings
   460→
   461→| Phase Topic                   | Likely Pitfall                                                                                 | Severity | Mitigation                                                                                                               |
   462→| ----------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
   463→| Document editor selection     | Choosing TipTap/Hocuspocus because of DX, then discovering it requires plaintext server access | Critical | Evaluate encryption compatibility before choosing an editor. Build a proof-of-concept with encrypted Yjs transport first |
   464→| CRDT integration with IPNS    | Autosave frequency conflicts with IPNS publish latency                                         | Critical | Separate real-time CRDT sync from IPNS persistence. Use a debounced save queue                                           |
   465→| Team vault key hierarchy      | Single team key without per-epoch rotation leads to forward secrecy violations                 | Critical | Design the key epoch system before building team features. Model after Keybase's per-team-key architecture               |
   466→| Team member removal           | Forgetting to rotate keys on member removal                                                    | Critical | Key rotation on member removal must be a hard requirement in the spec, not an enhancement                                |
   467→| Admin recovery                | Adding server-side recovery key that breaks ZK                                                 | Critical | Design recovery as ECIES-wrapped-for-admin, never plaintext on server                                                    |
   468→| Billing webhook handling      | Non-idempotent webhook processing causing state corruption                                     | Moderate | Implement event deduplication from day one                                                                               |
   469→| Billing enforcement           | Server cannot inspect content to enforce quotas                                                | Moderate | Enforce quotas at API level (upload size, pin count) not content level                                                   |
   470→| Document signing legal claims | Claiming legal validity without proper identity verification or TSA                            | Moderate | Label as "cryptographic attestation" unless proper PKI is integrated                                                     |
   471→| Spreadsheet scope             | Treating sheets as "just another editor"                                                       | Moderate | Scope M3 to documents only, or budget 3x for sheets/slides                                                               |
   472→| Large document performance    | Encrypting full document state on every save                                                   | Moderate | Use incremental (diff-based) encryption and Web Workers                                                                  |
   473→
   474→---
   475→
   476→## Anti-Patterns to Avoid
   477→
   478→### "Just Encrypt the Yjs Updates on the Server"
   479→
   480→**Wrong:** Encrypt CRDT operations at the server before storing them.
   481→**Why wrong:** The server has seen the plaintext operations. Encryption at rest does not help if the server processed plaintext in transit.
   482→**Right:** Encrypt on the client before sending. The server only ever sees ciphertext.
   483→
   484→### "Use a Shared Password for the Team"
   485→
   486→**Wrong:** Team members share a password that derives a symmetric key.
   487→**Why wrong:** Password cannot be rotated without disrupting all members. No way to revoke a single member's access. Password strength is the weakest link.
   488→**Right:** Each member's access is gated by their own ECIES public key wrapping a per-team key.
   489→
   490→### "Let the Server Enforce Document Permissions"
   491→
   492→**Wrong:** Server checks user roles before decrypting and serving document content.
   493→**Why wrong:** Server cannot decrypt document content (ZK). Permission enforcement must happen at the key distribution level: if a user does not have the wrapped key for a document, they cannot decrypt it, regardless of server-side role checks.
   494→**Right:** Permissions are enforced cryptographically. Access = having the key. Server-side role checks are a defense-in-depth layer, not the primary access control.
   495→
   496→### "Stripe Is Our Source of Truth for Access"
   497→
   498→**Wrong:** Check Stripe subscription status on every API request to determine if the user can access their vault.
   499→**Why wrong:** Stripe API latency (100-500ms) on every request is unacceptable. Stripe rate limits will be hit. If Stripe is down, all users lose access.
   500→**Right:** Sync Stripe state to a local subscription table via webhooks. Query the local table for access checks. Reconcile periodically.
   501→
   502→---
   503→
   504→## Open Questions Needing Deeper Research
   505→
   506→1. **CRDT compaction in encrypted context:** Can Yjs checkpoints be created without the server seeing the document state? How does compaction interact with the IPNS content-addressed storage model?
   507→
   508→2. **Real-time collaboration latency budget:** What is the acceptable latency for encrypted CRDT operations? Users expect sub-100ms keystroke propagation; encryption adds overhead. Is this achievable with Web Workers + AES-GCM?
   509→
   510→3. **IPNS record size limits:** As team metadata grows (N members x M wrapped keys), do IPNS records have practical size limits? What happens when a team vault's metadata exceeds the IPNS record payload?
   511→
   512→4. **Offline editing with teams:** How do offline edits work when the team key has been rotated while a member was offline? The member's local CRDT state is encrypted with the old key; they need the new key to publish.
   513→
   514→5. **Cross-document references:** In spreadsheets, formulas can reference other sheets/documents. In an encrypted system, this requires access to multiple decryption keys simultaneously. How does this interact with the per-folder key hierarchy?
   515→
   516→---
   517→
   518→## Sources
   519→
   520→- [CryptPad Architecture](https://github.com/cryptpad/cryptpad/blob/main/docs/ARCHITECTURE.md) -- encrypted collaboration architecture reference
   521→- [ChainPad Algorithm](https://github.com/cryptpad/chainpad) -- CRDT/blockchain-based collaborative editing
   522→- [Keybase Team Crypto](https://book.keybase.io/docs/teams/crypto) -- team key management with per-team keys and rotation
   523→- [Proton Docs Introduction](https://proton.me/blog/docs-proton-drive) -- E2EE collaborative document editing challenges
   524→- [Yjs Documentation](https://docs.yjs.dev/) -- CRDT implementation, subdocuments, and providers
   525→- [Stripe Webhook Documentation](https://docs.stripe.com/billing/subscriptions/webhooks) -- idempotent webhook processing
   526→- [Stripe PCI Compliance Guide](https://stripe.com/guides/pci-compliance) -- payment security requirements
   527→- [ESIGN Act and UETA](https://www.docusign.com/blog/are-electronic-signatures-legal) -- e-signature legal requirements
   528→- [Tresorit Security](https://tresorit.com/security) -- ZK team key management reference
   529→- [Bitwarden Zero-Knowledge Encryption](https://bitwarden.com/resources/zero-knowledge-encryption-white-paper/) -- ZK architecture patterns
   530→- [ChainSafe E2E Encrypted Collaborative Editing](https://research.chainsafe.io/featured/Publications/E2E-Encrypted-Doc/) -- academic research on encrypted CRDT collaboration
   531→- [DigiCert Timestamping Best Practices](https://www.digicert.com/blog/best-practices-timestamping) -- TSA integration for document signing
   532→

     1→# Technology Stack: Milestone 4
     2→
     3→**Project:** CipherBox - Encrypted Productivity Suite
     4→**Researched:** 2026-02-11
     5→**Scope:** Document/Spreadsheet/Presentation editors, Team accounts, Billing, Document signing
     6→
     7→## Executive Summary
     8→
     9→M3 transforms CipherBox from encrypted file storage into an encrypted productivity suite.
    10→The core constraint remains: all content is client-side encrypted and the server never sees
    11→plaintext. This constrains every technology choice -- editors must operate entirely in-browser
    12→on decrypted content, collaboration must use E2EE CRDTs (not server-mediated OT), and document
    13→signing must work with client-side cryptographic primitives.
    14→
    15→The recommended stack centers on TipTap 3.x (rich text), Univer (spreadsheets), a custom
    16→TipTap-based slide editor (presentations), CASL (authorization), Stripe + NOWPayments
    17→(billing), and LibPDF + Web Crypto (document signing).
    18→
    19→---
    20→
    21→## 1. Document Editor (Rich Text)
    22→
    23→### Recommended: TipTap 3.x + Yjs
    24→
    25→| Package                                  | Version | Purpose                                                    |
    26→| ---------------------------------------- | ------- | ---------------------------------------------------------- |
    27→| `@tiptap/react`                          | ^3.19.0 | React integration for TipTap editor                        |
    28→| `@tiptap/starter-kit`                    | ^3.19.0 | Bundle of essential extensions (bold, italic, lists, etc.) |
    29→| `@tiptap/extension-collaboration`        | ^3.19.0 | Yjs-based CRDT collaboration                               |
    30→| `@tiptap/extension-collaboration-cursor` | ^3.19.0 | Shared cursor awareness                                    |
    31→| `@tiptap/extension-table`                | ^3.19.0 | Table editing support                                      |
    32→| `@tiptap/extension-image`                | ^3.19.0 | Inline image support                                       |
    33→| `@tiptap/extension-placeholder`          | ^3.19.0 | Placeholder text                                           |
    34→| `@tiptap/extension-text-align`           | ^3.19.0 | Text alignment                                             |
    35→| `@tiptap/extension-underline`            | ^3.19.0 | Underline formatting                                       |
    36→| `@tiptap/extension-color`                | ^3.19.0 | Text color                                                 |
    37→| `@tiptap/extension-highlight`            | ^3.19.0 | Text highlighting                                          |
    38→| `yjs`                                    | ^13.x   | CRDT data structure for conflict-free sync                 |
    39→| `y-indexeddb`                            | ^9.x    | Offline persistence for Yjs documents                      |
    40→
    41→**Confidence:** HIGH -- TipTap 3.0 is stable (confirmed Feb 2026), actively maintained, and
    42→the de facto standard for React rich text editors.
    43→
    44→**Why TipTap over alternatives:**
    45→
    46→| Editor            | Why Not                                                                               |
    47→| ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
    48→| Slate.js          | Lower-level API, more work to build equivalent UX. No built-in collaboration.         |
    49→| Quill             | Older architecture, less extensible, weaker TypeScript support.                       |
    50→| Lexical (Meta)    | Newer but smaller extension ecosystem. Less community adoption for production apps.   |
    51→| CKEditor 5        | Proprietary collaboration server. GPL license requires open-sourcing derivative work. |
    52→| ProseMirror (raw) | TipTap IS ProseMirror with a better DX layer. No reason to go lower.                  |
    53→
    54→**Zero-knowledge architecture fit:**
    55→
    56→TipTap + Yjs is uniquely suited for CipherBox because:
    57→
    58→1. Yjs is a CRDT -- it does not require a central server to mediate changes (unlike OT).
    59→2. Yjs document state is a binary blob (`Y.encodeStateAsUpdate()`) that can be
    60→   AES-256-GCM encrypted before storage/transmission.
    61→3. The Yjs E2EE pattern is documented and used in production (Proton Docs uses Yjs with E2EE).
    62→4. Offline-first: y-indexeddb persists the encrypted Yjs doc locally; sync happens
    63→   when peers reconnect.
    64→
    65→**Integration with existing stack:**
    66→
    67→- Yjs doc binary is encrypted with AES-256-GCM using a per-document `fileKey` (same
    68→  pattern as existing file encryption).
    69→- Encrypted Yjs state is stored as an IPFS blob (same as files).
    70→- Document metadata (name, type, lastModified) lives in the folder IPNS record.
    71→- No server-side changes needed for basic single-user editing. Team collaboration
    72→  requires a thin WebSocket relay (see Architecture note below).
    73→
    74→### Collaboration Relay (for team editing)
    75→
    76→| Package                | Version | Purpose                                |
    77→| ---------------------- | ------- | -------------------------------------- |
    78→| `@hocuspocus/server`   | ^3.4.4  | WebSocket server for Yjs sync          |
    79→| `@hocuspocus/provider` | ^3.4.4  | Client-side WebSocket provider for Yjs |
    80→
    81→**Architecture note:** For team collaboration, Hocuspocus acts as a _dumb relay_. It forwards
    82→encrypted Yjs update blobs between connected clients. The server never decrypts content.
    83→Clients decrypt updates using the shared document key (distributed via ECIES re-wrapping,
    84→same as M2 file sharing). Hocuspocus hooks validate authentication and authorization but
    85→never touch plaintext.
    86→
