    89→## 2. Spreadsheet Editor
    90→
    91→### Recommended: Univer Sheets
    92→
    93→| Package                       | Version | Purpose                              |
    94→| ----------------------------- | ------- | ------------------------------------ |
    95→| `@univerjs/core`              | ^0.15.x | Core engine                          |
    96→| `@univerjs/design`            | ^0.15.x | Design system / theming              |
    97→| `@univerjs/engine-render`     | ^0.15.x | Canvas rendering engine              |
    98→| `@univerjs/engine-formula`    | ^0.15.x | Formula calculation engine           |
    99→| `@univerjs/sheets`            | ^0.15.x | Spreadsheet data model               |
   100→| `@univerjs/sheets-ui`         | ^0.15.x | Spreadsheet UI components            |
   101→| `@univerjs/sheets-formula`    | ^0.15.x | Formula integration                  |
   102→| `@univerjs/sheets-formula-ui` | ^0.15.x | Formula bar UI                       |
   103→| `@univerjs/sheets-numfmt`     | ^0.15.x | Number formatting                    |
   104→| `@univerjs/sheets-numfmt-ui`  | ^0.15.x | Number format UI                     |
   105→| `@univerjs/docs`              | ^0.15.x | Required dependency for cell editing |
   106→| `@univerjs/docs-ui`           | ^0.15.x | Required dependency for cell editing |
   107→| `@univerjs/ui`                | ^0.15.x | Generic UI framework                 |
   108→
   109→**Confidence:** MEDIUM -- Univer is the most capable open-source spreadsheet framework but
   110→is still pre-1.0 (0.15.x). APIs may change between minor versions. However, it is actively
   111→maintained with releases every few days.
   112→
   113→**Why Univer over alternatives:**
   114→
   115→| Library           | Why Not                                                                                |
   116→| ----------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
   117→| FortuneSheet      | Smaller community, fewer features (no conditional formatting, limited formulas).       |
   118→| Handsontable      | Commercial license required for production. Not truly open source.                     |
   119→| react-spreadsheet | Too simple -- no formula engine, no formatting, no charts. Data grid, not spreadsheet. |
   120→| AG Grid           | Data grid, not spreadsheet. No formula bar, cell merging, etc.                         |
   121→| Jspreadsheet CE   | jQuery-era architecture, weak TypeScript support.                                      |
   122→
   123→**Zero-knowledge architecture fit:**
   124→
   125→- Univer operates entirely client-side. The spreadsheet data model is a JSON structure
   126→  that can be serialized, encrypted with AES-256-GCM, and stored as an IPFS blob.
   127→- `JSON.stringify(univerInstance.save())` produces the complete spreadsheet state.
   128→- On load: fetch encrypted blob from IPFS, decrypt, `JSON.parse()`, hydrate Univer.
   129→- Formula computation happens client-side -- server never needs access.
   130→
   131→**Collaboration caveat:**
   132→
   133→Univer's built-in collaboration uses OT (Operational Transformation), which requires a
   134→server to mediate. This conflicts with CipherBox's zero-knowledge model. For M3, recommend
   135→**single-user editing only** for spreadsheets. Multi-user spreadsheet collaboration would
   136→require either:
   137→
   138→- Building a Yjs binding for Univer (significant effort, no existing solution)
   139→- Using Univer's OT in a TEE enclave (complex, possibly over-engineered for a demo)
   140→
   141→**Recommendation:** Ship spreadsheets as single-user-editable in M3. If team spreadsheet
   142→editing becomes a priority, evaluate building a Yjs adapter in a future milestone.
   143→

   157→## 4. Team Accounts and Authorization
   158→
   159→### Recommended: CASL + Custom NestJS Guards
   160→
   161→| Package         | Version | Side     | Purpose                                    |
   162→| --------------- | ------- | -------- | ------------------------------------------ |
   163→| `@casl/ability` | ^6.7.5  | Shared   | Define and check permissions               |
   164→| `@casl/react`   | ^4.0.0  | Frontend | React components for conditional rendering |
   165→| `nest-casl`     | ^1.9.15 | Backend  | NestJS integration for CASL                |
   166→
   167→**Confidence:** HIGH -- CASL is the standard authorization library for the Node.js/NestJS
   168→ecosystem. Well-documented, actively maintained, and explicitly designed for RBAC/ABAC.
   169→
   170→**Why CASL over alternatives:**
   171→
   172→| Alternative           | Why Not                                                                             |
   173→| --------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
   174→| Custom guards only    | Reinventing the wheel. CASL provides a proven, testable abstraction.                |
   175→| casbin                | More complex (policy language), designed for microservices. Overkill for CipherBox. |
   176→| NestJS built-in roles | Too simple. No field-level permissions, no conditions, no client-side sharing.      |
   177→
   178→**Role model for CipherBox teams:**
   179→
   180→| Role     | Permissions                                                |
   181→| -------- | ---------------------------------------------------------- |
   182→| `owner`  | All operations, billing, member management, delete org     |
   183→| `admin`  | All operations except billing and org deletion             |
   184→| `member` | Create, read, update, delete own files. Read shared files. |
   185→| `viewer` | Read-only access to shared files/folders                   |
   186→
   187→**Database additions (TypeORM entities):**
   188→
   189→```text
   190→Organization { id, name, createdAt, updatedAt }
   191→OrganizationMember { orgId, userId, role, invitedAt, joinedAt }
   192→OrganizationInvite { orgId, email, role, token, expiresAt }
   193→```
   194→
   195→**Zero-knowledge implications:**
   196→
   197→Team key management extends M2's sharing model:
   198→
   199→- Each organization has an `orgFolderKey` encrypted to each member's public key (ECIES).
   200→- When a member is added, the org owner re-wraps the `orgFolderKey` to the new member's
   201→  public key.
   202→- When a member is removed, key rotation is triggered: new `orgFolderKey`, re-encrypt
   203→  all org folder metadata, re-wrap to remaining members.
   204→- The server stores encrypted keys only. It enforces role-based API access but never
   205→  sees plaintext content.
   206→
   207→---
   208→
   209→## 5. Billing Integration
   210→
   211→### 5A. Stripe (Traditional Payments)
   212→
   213→| Package                    | Version | Side     | Purpose                               |
   214→| -------------------------- | ------- | -------- | ------------------------------------- |
   215→| `stripe`                   | ^20.3.1 | Backend  | Stripe API SDK                        |
   216→| `@golevelup/nestjs-stripe` | ^0.9.3  | Backend  | NestJS module with webhook autowiring |
   217→| `@stripe/stripe-js`        | ^5.x    | Frontend | Stripe.js loader                      |
   218→| `@stripe/react-stripe-js`  | ^5.6.0  | Frontend | React Elements components             |
   219→
   220→**Confidence:** HIGH -- Stripe is the industry standard. The NestJS integration via
   221→`@golevelup/nestjs-stripe` handles webhook verification and routing automatically.
   222→
   223→**Integration approach:**
   224→
   225→- Use Stripe Checkout (hosted payment page) for initial subscriptions. Minimizes PCI scope.
   226→- Use Stripe Customer Portal for subscription management (upgrade, downgrade, cancel).
   227→- Webhook handler processes `checkout.session.completed`, `invoice.paid`,
   228→  `customer.subscription.updated`, `customer.subscription.deleted`.
   229→- Store `stripeCustomerId` and `stripeSubscriptionId` on the User/Organization entity.
   230→- Feature gating: check subscription tier in NestJS guards alongside CASL permissions.
   231→
   232→**Database additions:**
   233→
   234→```text
   235→Subscription { id, orgId, stripeCustomerId, stripeSubscriptionId, tier, status, currentPeriodEnd }
   236→```
   237→
   238→### 5B. Crypto Payments (Privacy-Preserving)
   239→
   240→| Package                             | Version | Side    | Purpose                |
   241→| ----------------------------------- | ------- | ------- | ---------------------- |
   242→| `@nowpaymentsio/nowpayments-api-js` | ^1.0.x  | Backend | NOWPayments API client |
   243→
   244→**Confidence:** MEDIUM -- NOWPayments is a legitimate service with an official npm package,
   245→but the package is less actively maintained than Stripe's SDK.
   246→
   247→**Why NOWPayments over alternatives:**
   248→
   249→| Alternative             | Why Not                                                                                  |
   250→| ----------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
   251→| BTCPay Server           | Requires self-hosting a Bitcoin full node. Heavy infrastructure for a demo.              |
   252→| CoinGate                | Similar to NOWPayments but smaller JS ecosystem.                                         |
   253→| Direct Web3 (ethers.js) | Requires building your own payment tracking, exchange rate handling, confirmation logic. |
   254→| Coinbase Commerce       | Coinbase is a centralized exchange -- undermines anonymity goal.                         |
   255→
   256→**Why NOWPayments:**
   257→
   258→- Hosted service -- no infrastructure to manage.
   259→- Supports 350+ cryptocurrencies including BTC, ETH, USDT, USDC.
   260→- REST API with IPN (Instant Payment Notification) webhooks.
   261→- No KYC required for the payer (preserves anonymity).
   262→- 1% fee (competitive with crypto payment processors).
   263→
   264→**Integration approach:**
   265→
   266→- Create a payment invoice via NOWPayments API when user selects crypto payment.
   267→- Display payment address and QR code to user.
   268→- Backend receives IPN webhook when payment is confirmed.
   269→- Map payment to subscription activation (same tier system as Stripe).
   270→- Crypto payments activate a fixed-duration subscription (e.g., 30 days per payment).
   271→
   272→#### Alternative Consideration: BTCPay Server
   273→
   274→If the project later needs maximum decentralization (no third-party payment processor),
   275→BTCPay Server is the gold standard. It is self-hosted, open-source, and zero-fee. However,
   276→it requires running a Bitcoin full node (~500GB disk) and optional Lightning node. This
   277→is too heavy for a demo but worth noting for a production roadmap.
   278→
   279→---
   280→
   281→## 6. Secure Document Signing
   282→
   283→### Recommended: LibPDF + Web Crypto API + react-signature-canvas
   284→
   285→| Package                  | Version       | Side               | Purpose                                       |
   286→| ------------------------ | ------------- | ------------------ | --------------------------------------------- |
   287→| `@libpdf/core`           | latest (beta) | Shared             | PDF parsing, modification, digital signatures |
   288→| `react-signature-canvas` | ^1.0.7        | Frontend           | Signature drawing pad                         |
   289→| `@signpdf/signpdf`       | ^3.3.0        | Backend (optional) | PDF signing with certificates                 |
   290→
   291→**Confidence:** MEDIUM-LOW for LibPDF (beta, APIs may change), HIGH for the overall approach.
   292→
   293→**Why LibPDF over alternatives:**
   294→
   295→| Alternative                     | Why Not                                                                                  |
   296→| ------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
   297→| pdf-lib                         | Last updated 4 years ago (v1.17.1). No digital signature support. No active maintenance. |
   298→| Apryse/Nutrient                 | Commercial. Expensive licensing.                                                         |
   299→| node-signpdf (@signpdf/signpdf) | Good for server-side signing but CipherBox needs client-side signing.                    |
   300→
   301→**Why LibPDF:**
   302→
   303→- TypeScript-first (designed for TS, not retrofitted).
   304→- Supports PAdES digital signatures (B-B through B-LTA).
   305→- Preserves existing digital signatures during modification (incremental saves).
   306→- Works in both Node.js and the browser from the same code.
   307→- Built by Documenso (the open-source DocuSign alternative) -- real production usage.
   308→- Under active development with frequent releases.
   309→
   310→**Zero-knowledge document signing approach:**
   311→
   312→CipherBox document signing is fundamentally different from DocuSign because documents
   313→are encrypted. The signing flow:
   314→
   315→1. User opens encrypted document in browser (decrypted client-side).
   316→2. User draws signature on `react-signature-canvas` or types/uploads signature image.
   317→3. Client generates a signing keypair (or uses their Web3Auth-derived key).
   318→4. Client creates a cryptographic signature over the document hash using Web Crypto API
   319→   (`crypto.subtle.sign` with the existing secp256k1 key via Web3Auth).
   320→5. The signature + signer's public key + timestamp are embedded in a signature metadata
   321→   record stored alongside the document.
   322→6. For PDF export: LibPDF embeds the digital signature into a rendered PDF.
   323→7. Verification: any party with the signer's public key can verify the signature
   324→   against the document hash.
   325→
   326→**Important distinction:** This is a _cryptographic_ signature (proving the signer had
   327→the private key), not just a visual e-signature overlay. CipherBox's existing key
   328→infrastructure (secp256k1 via Web3Auth) makes this natural.
   329→
   330→**Database additions:**
   331→
   332→```text
   333→DocumentSignature { id, documentId, signerPublicKey, signatureHex, documentHash, signedAt, metadata }
   334→SignatureRequest { id, documentId, requesterId, signerEmail, status, expiresAt }
   335→```
   336→
   337→---
   338→
   339→## 7. Supporting Infrastructure
   340→
   341→### WebSocket Server (for collaboration relay)
   342→
   343→The existing NestJS backend should be extended with a WebSocket gateway for Hocuspocus.
   344→
   345→| Package               | Version | Side    | Purpose                  |
   346→| --------------------- | ------- | ------- | ------------------------ |
   347→| `@nestjs/websockets`  | ^11.x   | Backend | NestJS WebSocket support |
   348→| `@nestjs/platform-ws` | ^11.x   | Backend | WS adapter for NestJS    |
   349→
   350→**Note:** Hocuspocus can run as a standalone process or be integrated into the NestJS
   351→application. For CipherBox, running it as a separate process behind the same reverse proxy
   352→is recommended to isolate long-lived WebSocket connections from the REST API.
   353→
   354→### Email (for invitations and signature requests)
   355→
   356→| Package                  | Version | Side    | Purpose                              |
   357→| ------------------------ | ------- | ------- | ------------------------------------ |
   358→| `@nestjs-modules/mailer` | ^2.x    | Backend | NestJS email integration             |
   359→| `nodemailer`             | ^6.x    | Backend | Email transport (peer dep of mailer) |
   360→
   361→**Note:** Required for team invitations and signature request notifications. Use a
   362→transactional email service (Resend, SendGrid, or AWS SES) as the transport.
   363→
   364→---
   365→
   366→## What NOT to Add
   367→
   368→| Technology                 | Why Not                                                                                                                                             |
   369→| -------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
   370→| Socket.IO                  | Hocuspocus uses native WebSockets via `ws`. Adding Socket.IO would duplicate functionality.                                                         |
   371→| MongoDB                    | Document editors do not need a separate document database. Yjs state is stored as encrypted blobs on IPFS. Relational metadata stays in PostgreSQL. |
   372→| Redis Pub/Sub for collab   | Hocuspocus handles multi-instance sync natively. BullMQ + Redis already exists for job queues.                                                      |
   373→| GraphQL                    | REST + OpenAPI (existing pattern) is sufficient. GraphQL adds complexity without clear benefit for CipherBox's API surface.                         |
   374→| Electron                   | Tauri v2 is already chosen for desktop. Do not add Electron.                                                                                        |
   375→| Firebase/Supabase Realtime | Third-party real-time databases conflict with zero-knowledge architecture.                                                                          |
   376→| OT-based collaboration     | Requires server to see document structure. Incompatible with E2EE. Use CRDTs (Yjs).                                                                 |
   377→
   378→---

   381→

   383→

   387→  @tiptap/extension-collaboration @tiptap/extension-collaboration-cursor \

   392→

   399→

   402→

   405→

   411→```
   412→
   413→### Backend (apps/api)
   414→
   415→```bash

   418→  @nowpaymentsio/nowpayments-api-js

   422→

   428→pnpm add -D @types/nodemailer
   429→```

   432→
   433→## Alternatives Considered (Full Matrix)
   434→
   435→| Category            | Recommended            | Runner-up          | Why Runner-up Lost                                            |
   436→| ------------------- | ---------------------- | ------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------- |
   437→| Rich text editor    | TipTap 3.x             | Lexical (Meta)     | Smaller extension ecosystem, less community proof             |
   438→| CRDT engine         | Yjs                    | Automerge          | Yjs is faster, larger ecosystem, TipTap integration built-in  |
   439→| Spreadsheet         | Univer                 | FortuneSheet       | Univer has formula engine, conditional formatting, active dev |
   440→| Slides              | Custom (TipTap)        | reveal.js wrapper  | reveal.js is a viewer, not an editor                          |
   441→| Authorization       | CASL                   | casbin             | casbin is over-engineered for single-app RBAC                 |
   442→| Traditional billing | Stripe                 | Paddle             | Stripe has better NestJS integration, larger ecosystem        |
   443→| Crypto billing      | NOWPayments            | BTCPay Server      | BTCPay requires self-hosted Bitcoin node                      |
   444→| PDF signing         | LibPDF                 | pdf-lib            | pdf-lib abandoned (4yr old), no digital signature support     |
   445→| Signature pad       | react-signature-canvas | SurveyJS signature | react-signature-canvas is focused, lightweight, typed         |

   469→## Confidence Assessment
   470→
   471→| Area                                | Confidence | Rationale                                                                      |
   472→| ----------------------------------- | ---------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
   473→| Document editor (TipTap + Yjs)      | HIGH       | Stable v3, proven E2EE CRDT pattern (Proton Docs), massive ecosystem           |

   476→| Team accounts (CASL)                | HIGH       | Standard library, well-documented NestJS patterns                              |
   477→| Stripe billing                      | HIGH       | Industry standard, actively maintained NestJS module                           |
   478→| Crypto billing (NOWPayments)        | MEDIUM     | Official JS SDK exists but less actively maintained than Stripe                |
   479→| Document signing (LibPDF)           | MEDIUM-LOW | Beta library, but TypeScript-first and used in production by Documenso         |
   480→| Document signing (overall approach) | HIGH       | Web Crypto API + secp256k1 is proven; LibPDF is the riskiest piece             |

  "old_string": "**For TEE republishes:** The TEE already has the decrypted IPNS private key in hardware. Instead of TEE signing a record and the API broadcasting via delegated routing, the TEE can return the raw Ed25519 private key bytes (still inside the TEE boundary), and the API can:\n\n1. Import the key temporarily into Kubo's keystore (`/api/v0/key/import`)\n2. Call `/api/v0/name/publish` directly through Kubo\n3. Remove the key from Kubo's keystore (`/api/v0/key/rm`)\n\nThis bypasses delegated-ipfs.dev entirely for republishing. However, this is an optimization, not a requirement. The existing TEE->signed record->delegated routing path still works as a fallback.\n\n**Security note on Kubo key import:** The IPNS private key is transiently present in Kubo's memory during the publish call. This is acceptable because:\n\n- Kubo already runs on the same host as the API in a Docker network\n- The key is encrypted at rest in the DB (encrypted with TEE public key)\n- The key is removed from Kubo's keystore immediately after publish\n- The alternative (delegated-ipfs.dev) sends the signed record over the internet, which is a larger attack surface",
  "new_string": "**For TEE republishes:** The existing flow is preserved: the TEE decrypts the IPNS private key in hardware, signs the IPNS record inside the TEE boundary, and returns the **signed record bytes** (not the raw key) to the API. The API then broadcasts the pre-signed record to the DHT via Kubo's `/api/v0/routing/put`, replacing delegated-ipfs.dev as the broadcast target.\n\nThe IPNS private key never leaves the TEE boundary. This maintains the security invariant that the API server never handles raw IPNS private keys.\n\n**Rejected alternative (Kubo key import):** An optimization was considered where the TEE returns raw Ed25519 private key bytes for temporary import into Kubo's keystore (`/api/v0/key/import` → `/api/v0/name/publish` → `/api/v0/key/rm`). This was rejected because it would export the private key from the trusted boundary, undermining the TEE security model even if the key is only transiently present in Kubo's memory."
}
```

It is NEVER sent to the CipherBox API -- the remote pinning calls are made by the NestJS backend using a token the user provides via an encrypted channel.\n\n**Alternative approach considered: Client-direct pinning.** The web app could call the user's pinning endpoint directly. This avoids the server seeing the access token but introduces CORS issues (most pinning services don't set `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` for arbitrary origins) and breaks desktop parity (Tauri apps don't have CORS restrictions but would need a different code path).\n\n**Recommendation: Server-relay for v1.1.** The server relays pin requests using an access token the user provides encrypted with the server's public key. The server sees the token but never the plaintext content (it only pins CIDs of encrypted blobs). Evaluate client-direct as a privacy upgrade in a future milestone.",
  "new_string": "The access token is provided by the user and stored server-side, encrypted with the server's public key. The NestJS backend decrypts the token to make remote pinning calls on the user's behalf.\n\n**Scoping decision: Server-relay for v1.1.** The server relays pin requests using the user's access token. The server sees the token but never the plaintext file content (it only pins CIDs of already-encrypted blobs). This tradeoff was explicitly accepted: the server knowing a pinning service credential is less sensitive than the server seeing file contents, and server-relay preserves quota tracking and conflict detection.\n\n**Alternative rejected: Client-direct pinning.** The web app could call the user's pinning endpoint directly, avoiding the server seeing the access token. Rejected for v1.1 because: CORS issues (most pinning services don't set `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` for arbitrary origins), breaks desktop parity, and bypasses server-side quota tracking and optimistic concurrency. Deferred as BYO-08 to v1.2."
}
```

Phase 3 (database minimization) moves `encryptedRootFolderKey` into an IPFS blob via a versioned vault blob v2 format, with dual-write migration and DB fallback retained for disaster recovery. Phase 4 (BYO-IPFS) adds a `RemotePinningProvider` speaking the standard IPFS Pinning Service API, with dual-pin strategy (always pin to CipherBox node, best-effort mirror to user's node).",
  "new_string": "The recommended approach is a five-phase execution with strict ordering (Phases 18-22). Phase 18 (performance instrumentation) is zero-risk and establishes server-side baselines before any architectural changes. Phase 19 (IPNS reliability) inverts the resolution model to DB-first with async Kubo DHT verification via self-hosted Someguy, eliminating the `delegated-ipfs.dev` dependency. Phase 20 (database minimization) moves `encryptedRootFolderKey` into an IPFS blob via a versioned vault blob v2 format, with dual-write migration and permanent DB fallback. Phase 21 (BYO-IPFS) adds a `RemotePinningProvider` speaking the standard IPFS Pinning Service API, with dual-pin strategy (always pin to CipherBox node, best-effort mirror to user's node). Phase 22 (performance baselines completion) adds client-side timing instrumentation, end-to-end journey timing, k6 load tests, and capacity documentation after all features are stable."
}

The research validates that the v1.0 architecture was designed with these features in mind: per-folder IPNS keypairs enable natural per-folder sharing, IPFS content-addressing makes versioning nearly free (stop unpinning old CIDs), and Web3Auth's threshold key scheme natively supports MFA. The dominant finding across all four research streams is that **most M2 features require zero new heavy dependencies**. They are protocol design and metadata schema problems, not technology selection problems. The existing cryptographic primitives (`eciesjs`, `@noble/*`, Web Crypto API) handle all required operations for sharing (ECIES re-wrapping), versioning (metadata extension), and search (client-side indexing).\n+CipherBox v1.1 is an infrastructure-hardening milestone, not a feature milestone. The four workstreams -- IPNS reliability, database minimization, BYO-IPFS, and performance baselines -- aim to transform CipherBox from \"IPFS as a storage backend with database fallbacks\" to \"IPFS-native with the database serving only coordination and auth.\" The dominant finding across all four research files is that the existing stack already has the capabilities needed. The Kubo v0.34.0 node can resolve IPNS records locally via its DHT; the `IpfsProvider` interface already abstracts pin/unpin/get for provider extension; `prom-client` is installed with an established `MetricsService` pattern; and TypeORM handles all necessary migrations. **Zero new npm dependencies are required for this entire milestone.**\n \n-The recommended approach is to build sharing around direct ECIES re-wrapping of folder keys per-recipient (the same pattern proven by Tresorit and ProtonDrive), implement versioning as a metadata schema extension that retains old CIDs instead of unpinning them, configure MFA through Web3Auth's built-in factor system rather than building a custom MFA layer, and implement search as a pure client-side operation against already-decrypted data in memory. **Read-only sharing must come first** -- this is the consensus across all four research tracks. Multi-writer IPNS is the single hardest unsolved problem and should be deferred entirely; no competitor has solved encrypted multi-writer folders either. The only genuinely new build target is the AWS Nitro TEE enclave (Rust binary), which is the highest-effort, highest-risk item and should ship last.\n+The recommended approach is a four-phase execution with strict ordering. Phase 1 (performance instrumentation) is zero-risk and establishes baselines before any architectural changes. Phase 2 (IPNS reliability) inverts the resolution model to DB-first with async Kubo DHT verification, eliminating the `delegated-ipfs.dev` dependency. Phase 3 (database minimization) moves `encryptedRootFolderKey` into an IPFS blob via a versioned vault blob v2 format, with dual-write migration and DB fallback retained for disaster recovery. Updated the summary to describe the correct 5-phase structure (Phases 18-22) with explicit phase numbers matching the roadmap.","created_at":"2026-03-07T03:58:49Z","updated_at":"2026-03-07T03:58:49Z","html_url":"https://github.com/FSM1/cipher-box/pull/280#discussion_r2898895116","pull_request_url":"https://api.github.com/repos/FSM1/cipher-box/pulls/280","_links":{"self":{"href":"https://api.github.com/repos/FSM1/cipher-box/pulls/comments/2898895116"},"html":{"href":"https://github.com/FSM1/cipher-box/pull/280#discussion_r2898895116"},"pull_request":{"href":"https://api.github.com/repos/FSM1/cipher-box/pulls/280"}},"reactions":{"url":"https://api.github.com/repos/FSM1/cipher-box/pulls/comments/2898895116/reactions","total_count":0,"+1":0,"-1":0,"laugh":0,"hooray":0,"confused":0,"heart":0,"rocket":0,"eyes":0},"start_line":null,"original_start_line":null,"start_side":null,"line":null,"original_line":12,"side":"RIGHT","in_reply_to_id":2898866920,"author_association":"OWNER","original_position":18,"position":1,"subject_type":"line"}{"url":"https://api.github.com/repos/FSM1/cipher-box/pulls/comments/2898895118","pull_request_review_id":3907265878,"id":2898895118,"node_id":"PRRC_kwDOQ6DXvM6syaEO","diff_hunk":"@@ -1,274 +1,187 @@\n # Project Research Summary\n \n-**Project:** CipherBox -- Milestone 2\n-**Domain:** Zero-knowledge encrypted cloud storage (sharing, search, MFA, versioning, advanced sync)\n-**Researched:** 2026-02-11\n-**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH\n+**Project:** CipherBox v1.1 -- IPFS Infrastructure\n+**Domain:** IPFS/IPNS reliability, database minimization, BYO-IPFS node support, performance baselines\n+**Researched:** 2026-03-07\n+**Confidence:** HIGH\n \n ## Executive Summary\n \n-Milestone 2 transforms CipherBox from a single-user encrypted storage demonstrator into a multi-user, feature-complete product. The research validates that the v1.0 architecture was designed with these features in mind: per-folder IPNS keypairs enable natural per-folder sharing, IPFS content-addressing makes versioning nearly free (stop unpinning old CIDs), and Web3Auth's threshold key scheme natively supports MFA. The dominant finding across all four research streams is that **most M2 features require zero new heavy dependencies**. They are protocol design and metadata schema problems, not technology selection problems. The existing cryptographic primitives (`eciesjs`, `@noble/*`, Web Crypto API) handle all required operations for sharing (ECIES re-wrapping), versioning (metadata extension), and search (client-side indexing).\n+CipherBox v1.1 is an infrastructure-hardening milestone, not a feature milestone. The four workstreams -- IPNS reliability, database minimization, BYO-IPFS, and performance baselines -- aim to transform CipherBox from \"IPFS as a storage backend with database fallbacks\" to \"IPFS-native with the database serving only coordination and auth.\" The dominant finding across all four research files is that the existing stack already has the capabilities needed. The Kubo v0.34.0 node can resolve IPNS records locally via its DHT; the `IpfsProvider` interface already abstracts pin/unpin/get for provider extension; `prom-client` is installed with an established `MetricsService` pattern; and TypeORM handles all necessary migrations. **Zero new npm dependencies are required for this entire milestone.**\n \n-The recommended approach is to build sharing around direct ECIES re-wrapping of folder keys per-recipient (the same pattern proven by Tresorit and ProtonDrive), implement versioning as a metadata schema extension that retains old CIDs instead of unpinning them, configure MFA through Web3Auth's built-in factor system rather than building a custom MFA layer, and implement search as a pure client-side operation against already-decrypted data in memory. **Read-only sharing must come first** -- this is the consensus across all four research tracks. Multi-writer IPNS is the single hardest unsolved problem and should be deferred entirely; no competitor has solved encrypted multi-writer folders either. The only genuinely new build target is the AWS Nitro TEE enclave (Rust binary), which is the highest-effort, highest-risk item and should ship last.\n+The recommended approach is a four-phase execution with strict ordering. Phase 1 (performance instrumentation) is zero-risk and establishes baselines before any architectural changes. Phase 2 (IPNS reliability) inverts the resolution model to DB-first with async Kubo DHT verification, eliminating the `delegated-ipfs.dev` dependency. Phase 3 (database minimization) moves `encryptedRootFolderKey` into an IPFS blob via a versioned vault blob v2 format, with dual-write migration and DB fallback retained for disaster recovery. Phase 4 (BYO-IPFS) adds a `RemotePinningProvider` speaking the standard IPFS Pinning Service API, with dual-pin strategy (always pin to CipherBox node, best-effort mirror to user's node).\n \n-The key risks are: (1) breaking zero-knowledge guarantees during sharing implementation (folderKey leaking to server in plaintext), (2) incomplete share revocation (forgetting to rotate the folderKey when revoking access), (3) MFA enrollment accidentally changing the derived keypair and locking users out of their vaults, and (4) IPNS last-writer-wins causing silent data loss in shared folders with multiple writers. All four are preventable with the specific mitigations documented in the pitfalls research -- the patterns are well-understood from Tresorit, ProtonDrive, and Filen precedents.\n+The primary risk is in Phase 3: moving rootFolderKey to IPFS creates a hard dependency on IPNS resolution for login. All four research files flag this independently. The mitigation is clear -- keep the DB copy as a permanent fallback, never make IPNS the sole access path for the root folder key. Secondary risks include sequence number divergence during routing provider migration (Phase 2) and quota tracking becoming unenforceable for BYO-IPFS users (Phase 4). Both have well-defined prevention strategies documented in PITFALLS.md.\n \n ## Key Findings\n \n ### Recommended Stack\n \n-The existing stack handles nearly everything. Only two lightweight client-side packages are truly new for the core feature set. The total bundle impact is under 10KB gzipped. Most Milestone 2 features need **ZERO new heavy dependencies**.\n+The milestone requires zero new npm dependencies. Every capability is built using existing libraries, configuration changes, and new provider implementations. The only external tooling addition is Grafana k6 v1.0 (a standalone Go binary, not an npm package) for load testing scripts.\n \n-**New dependencies (confirmed needed):**\n+**Core technologies (all existing):**\n \n-- `minisearch` ^7.2.0: Client-side full-text search engine -- TypeScript-native, 7KB gzip, zero deps, inverted index with O(log n) queries. Chosen over Fuse.js (O(n) per query) and FlexSearch (stale TS types).\n-- `idb` ^8.0.0: Promise-based IndexedDB wrapper -- 1KB gzip. For persisting encrypted search index and offline operation queue. Chosen over localForage (unnecessary fallback logic).\n-\n-**Conditional dependencies (only if implementing CipherBox-layer MFA beyond Web3Auth):**\n-\n-- `@simplewebauthn/browser` ^13.2.2 + `@simplewebauthn/server` ^13.2.2: WebAuthn passkey support -- TypeScript-first, actively maintained. Only if adding CipherBox-layer session MFA.\n-- `otpauth` ^9.5.0: TOTP generation/validation -- zero deps, RFC compliant. Only if CipherBox-level TOTP.\n-- `@scure/bip39` ^1.4.0: BIP-39 mnemonic generation -- same `@noble` ecosystem. Only if custom recovery phrase outside Web3Auth.\n-\n-**Deferred dependencies (AWS Nitro TEE):**\n-\n-- `@aws-sdk/client-kms` ^3.x: For NestJS orchestrator on parent EC2 instance.\n-- Rust enclave binary (separate build target): `aws-nitro-enclaves-nsm-api`, `ecies`, `ed25519-dalek`.\n-\n-**What NOT to add (and why):**\n-\n-| Library                   | Temptation               | Why Not                                                                                                                                      |\n-| ------------------------- | ------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |\n-| Yjs / Automerge / SecSync | \"CRDTs solve sync\"       | CipherBox syncs encrypted metadata blobs, not collaborative documents. CRDTs add 50KB+ and require restructuring the entire metadata format. |\n-| Elasticsearch / Typesense | \"Full-text search\"       | Server-side search violates zero-knowledge. All search must be client-side.                                                                  |\n-| Fuse.js                   | \"Fuzzy search\"           | No inverted index (O(n) per query). MiniSearch is materially faster.                                                                         |\n-| localForage               | \"IndexedDB wrapper\"      | Unnecessary fallback logic. `idb` is lighter and more appropriate.                                                                           |\n-| Proxy re-encryption libs  | \"Re-encrypt for sharing\" | Direct ECIES re-wrapping with existing `eciesjs` is simpler and sufficient.                                                                  |\n-| jsonwebtoken              | \"JWT for share tokens\"   | `jose` already installed, supports all JWK/JWS/JWE operations.                                                                               |\n-| passport-simple-webauthn  | \"Passport + WebAuthn\"    | v0.1.0, 2 years stale. Use `@simplewebauthn/server` directly.                                                                                |\n+- **Kubo v0.34.0 (recommend upgrade to v0.40.1):** Self-hosted IPFS node already participating in the Amino DHT. Supports `Gateway.ExposeRoutingAPI` and `Ipns.UsePubsub` for local IPNS resolution. v0.40.1 makes routing API default and improves IPNS-over-PubSub.\n+- **`prom-client` ^15.1.3:** Already installed with `MetricsService` pattern established. Extend with 4 new histograms for IPFS/IPNS latency tracking.\n+- **TypeORM ^0.3.28 + PostgreSQL 16.x:** Handles all migration needs. 2-3 new migrations (ADD COLUMN, ALTER COLUMN, CREATE TABLE for user IPFS config).\n+- **IPFS Pinning Service API (HTTP standard):** Vendor-agnostic OpenAPI spec with 4 endpoints. Implemented by Pinata, Filebase, web3.storage, IPFS Cluster. No SDK needed -- native `fetch` is sufficient.\n+- **k6 v1.0+ (dev tooling only):** Standalone load test runner with native TypeScript support. Install via `brew install k6`. Not committed to repo.\n \n ### Expected Features\n \n **Must have (table stakes):**\n \n-- User-to-user folder sharing (read-only) with ECIES key re-wrapping -- every competitor has this\n-- Share invitation flow (invite by email/publicKey, accept/decline)\n-- Share revocation with folderKey rotation -- critical security requirement\n-- File name search (global, across all folders) -- expected once vault exceeds 100+ files\n-- Web3Auth MFA enablement (device share + backup phrase) -- mostly SDK configuration\n-- Automatic version creation on file update (retain old CIDs) -- nearly free on IPFS\n-- Version history view with restore capability\n-- Conflict detection (IPNS sequence-based)\n-- Sync status indicators\n+- Reliable IPNS resolution (<2s, >99.5% availability) -- current `delegated-ipfs.dev` has documented 502s and stale records\n+- DB-cached CID fallback with sequence number comparison -- already implemented, needs to become the primary resolution path\n+- IPNS publish/resolve latency monitoring -- counters exist, need duration histograms\n+- API endpoint response time baselines with p50/p95/p99 targets\n+- Graceful degradation when IPFS/IPNS is slow -- timeout + fallback pattern, partially implemented\n \n **Should have (differentiators):**\n \n-- Link-based sharing with URL fragment key (Tresorit/MEGA pattern, no account required)\n-- Password-protected share links (PBKDF2 key derivation)\n-- IPFS-native version immutability (cryptographic tamper-proof versions for free)\n-- Per-folder granular sharing (share subfolder without exposing parent -- better than Tresorit's tresor-level)\n-- WebAuthn/Passkey MFA factor\n-- Selective sync for desktop (per-folder IPNS makes this architecturally natural)\n+- Move rootFolderKey to IPFS vault record -- server stores zero crypto material, true zero-knowledge relay\n+- BYO-IPFS node support via Pinning Service API -- unique among encrypted storage apps, enables self-sovereignty
